Non-uniform bounds and Edgeworth expansions in self-normalized limit theorems
[

Abstract
We study Edgeworth expansions in limit theorems for self-normalized sums. Non-uniform bounds for expansions in the central limit theorem are established while only imposing minimal moment conditions. Within this result, we address the case of non-integer moments leading to a reduced remainder. Furthermore, we provide non-uniform bounds for expansions in local limit theorems. The enhanced tail-accuracy of our non-uniform bounds allows for deriving an Edgeworth-type expansion in the entropic central limit theorem as well as a central limit theorem in total variation distance for self-normalized sums.



A]Pascal Beckedorf
and A]Angelika Rohde

??
Albert-Ludwigs-Universität Freiburg

??
??;
??
??

Contents

1
Introduction and main results

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent, identically distributed random variables with mean
\mathbb{E}X_{1}=0
and variance
\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}X_{1}=1
. Here and throughout this article,

S_{n}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j},\qquad V_{n}=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j}^{2}},\qquad T_{n}=\begin{cases}S_{n}/V_{n}&\text{ if }V_{n}>0\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

The self-normalized sum
T_{n}
was shown to be asymptotically normal if and only if
X_{1}
belongs to the domain of attraction of the normal law in
[
25
]
. This result has been generalized in
[
19
]
.

The distance of the distribution of
T_{n}
to a normal distribution has been investigated in various forms such as Berry–Esseen bounds
[
4
,
24
,
29
,
45
]
and non-uniform Berry–Esseen bounds
[
18
,
33
,
40
,
46
]
, to mention just a few. For an extensive survey on further self-normalized limit theorems we refer to
[
43
]
.

Concerning asymptotic expansions of the distribution of self-normalized sums, Chung
[
20
]
was the first to prove an Edgeworth expansion for the Student
t
-statistic. Bhattacharya and Ghosh
[
6
]
provided an approach of Edgeworth expansion including self-normalized sums, relaxing Chung’s moment conditions. The moment and smoothness assumptions of
[
6
]
were further weakened in
[
2
,
7
]
.
[
28
]
attained an expansion for the
t
-statistic under minimal moment conditions. One-term Edgeworth expansions were treated in
[
5
,
10
,
11
,
31
,
35
,
39
]
. Recently,
[
9
]
used the first two expansion terms to derive a sharper bound in a Berry–Esseen type result. Although not explicitly stated for self-normalized sums, Edgeworth expansions for densities of more general statistics are given in
[
30
, Section 2.8]
.

For normalized sums
Z_{n}=S_{n}/\sqrt{n}
, Edgeworth expansions with non-integer moment conditions and non-uniform bounds have been investigated for a long time in the CLT and LLT (see e.g.
[
8
,
38
]
). In case of non-integer moments, a CLT for
Z_{n}
has been derived in
[
12
]
using fractional calculus. These stronger results lay the foundation for limit theorems in more powerful metrics such as expansions in the entropic CLT
[
13
]
or related results for other information-theoretic distances
[
14
,
15
,
16
,
17
]
.

For
self-normalized sums
however, non-uniform bounds for Edgeworth expansions have not been studied so far. The main aim of this article is to derive such non-uniform bounds in the CLT and in the LLT for an Edgeworth expansion. These provide substantially stronger accuracy in the tails, enabling us to prove an entropic CLT and a CLT in total variation for self-normalized sums. As compared to normalized sums, the crucial obstacle here is the missing product form of the statistic’s characteristic function. Besides, we also treat the case of non-integer moments in the CLT. Subsequently, we give an overview on our results.

Let
\Phi
and
\phi
denote the standard normal distribution function and density function, respectively. Write
\mu_{k}=\mathbb{E}X_{1}^{k}
and
F_{n}(x)=\mathbb{P}(T_{n}\leq x)
for
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\lfloor x\rfloor
denote the integer part of
x
. With
\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}
we denote the Edgeworth expansion

\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)=\Phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}Q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2},\quad x\in\mathbb{R},

(1.1)

with
m-2
expansion terms of the distribution of
T_{n}
. Throughout this article,
X_{1}
is assumed to be symmetric such that all uneven moments vanish and thus
Q_{r}=0
for uneven
r\in\mathbb{N}
. In contrast to the approximation functions of
Z_{n}
, the polynomials
Q_{r}
generally do not have a closed form. However, for even
r
,
Q_{r}
provide a useful form which consist of
\phi
multiplied with an uneven polynomial of degree
2r-1
in
x
. The coefficients of
Q_{r}
are functions of the moments
\mu_{3},\dots,\mu_{r+2}
. The first approximating functions for the distribution function have the form

\begin{split}Q_{2}(x)&=-\phi(x)H_{3}(x)\big(-\tfrac{1}{12}\big)\mu_{4},\\
Q_{4}(x)&=-\phi(x)\Big(H_{7}(x)\tfrac{1}{288}\mu_{4}^{2}+H_{5}(x)\tfrac{1}{45}\mu_{6}+H_{3}(x)\tfrac{1}{12}\big(2\mu_{6}+\mu_{4}-3\mu_{4}^{2}\big)\Big),\end{split}

(1.2)

where
H_{k}
is the
k
-th Hermite polynomial.

Unless stated otherwise, all orders of convergence or divergence in
n
are understood for
n\to\infty
. Our first result is

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{s}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
. Then for
m=\lfloor{s}\rfloor
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|F_{n}(x)-\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+2m)/2}\big).

(1.3)

Note that only the moments used in the expansion have to be finite, thus we only pose minimal moment conditions. The distribution of
X_{1}
being non-singular (with respect to the Lebesgue measure) is only slightly stronger than Cramér’s condition

\limsup_{|t|\to\infty}|\mathbb{E}\exp(itX_{1})|<1

(1.4)

which is usually imposed when working with
Z_{n}
(see e.g.
[
8
,
38
]
).
Due to the denominator
V_{n}
, the characteristic function of
T_{n}
lacks the typical product form, required for an Edgeworth expansion. To overcome this problem, we first condition on
\mathcal{F}_{n}=\sigma(|X_{1}|,\dots,|X_{n}|)
as in
[
28
]
. On the one hand, the independence of the factors then provides a desired structure as a product. On the other hand, the random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
are discrete (non-lattice) random variables that are not identically distributed any longer. Therefore, the necessary Cramér condition
1.4
is not fulfilled and the standard theorems for Edgeworth expansions (such as from
[
8
,
38
]
) do not apply. These properties necessitate adjustments in the derivation of an Edgeworth expansion, but lead to rather involved integral remainder terms which have to be bounded thoroughly. At this point, the non-singularity is getting essential.

If
T_{n}
has a density, then by taking derivatives in
1.1
, the Edgeworth expansion of the density of
T_{n}
has the form

\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)=\phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}

(1.5)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. By differentiating
1.2
, the first approximating functions are

\begin{split}q_{2}(x)&=\phi(x)H_{4}(x)\big(-\tfrac{1}{12}\big)\mu_{4},\\
q_{4}(x)&=\phi(x)\Big(H_{8}(x)\tfrac{1}{288}\mu_{4}^{2}+H_{6}(x)\tfrac{1}{45}\mu_{6}+H_{4}(x)\tfrac{1}{12}\big(2\mu_{6}+\mu_{4}-3\mu_{4}^{2}\big)\Big).\end{split}

(1.6)

In the case that
r
is even,
q_{r}
provides a useful form which constitutes of
\phi
multiplied with an even polynomial of degree
2r
in
x
. The coefficients of
q_{r}
are functions of the moments
\mu_{3},\dots,\mu_{r+2}
. As above,
q_{r}=0
for uneven
r\in\mathbb{N}
.

Our second result is an application of our
Theorem

4.1
which has weaker but more technical conditions. Further LLTs with more formal but less stringent conditions are provided in
Section

4
. For the classical normalized statistic
Z_{n}
, such a result has been derived in
[
12
]
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, has a bounded density and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 3
. Then there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N
,
T_{n}
have densities
f_{n}
that satisfy

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-3)/2}\big).

Moreover, if
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m+2}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N},m\geq 2
, the order of convergence reduces to
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\,\log n\big)
.

The route of the proof of
Theorem

1.2
is related to the proof of
Theorem

1.1
. However, conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, the self-normalized sum
T_{n}
does not have a density as it is discrete. In particular, the Fourier inversion formula is not available any longer. The idea is to tackle this severe obstacle by perturbing
T_{n}
with an independent normal random variable with suitably chosen variance. Besides enjoying the properties that tails of density and characteristic function decay in the same magnitude, a Gaussian perturbation does not hinder convergence to the normal distribution. This perturbation trick allows to work with densities and deriving an Edgeworth expansion but comes at the cost of a deconvolution after returning to the unconditional setting. For performing a tight approximation in the final deconvolution step, verifiable conditions have to be imposed that guarantee sufficient smoothness of the density of
T_{n}
.

Based on
Theorem

1.2
, we prove an entropic CLT and its rates of convergence. For a random variable
X
with density
p
, mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
we define its entropy (with
0\log 0:=0
)

\displaystyle h(X)=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}p(x)\log p(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,

and its relative entropy

\displaystyle D(X)=D(X\|Z)=h(Z)-h(X)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}p(x)\log\frac{p(x)}{\phi_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x,

(1.7)

where
Z
is
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})
-distributed with density
\phi_{\mu,\sigma^{2}}
. As the normal distribution maximizes the entropy for given mean and variance, we know
D(X)\geq 0
.

Barron
[
3
]
was the first to prove an entropic CLT for the classical statistic
Z_{n}
, i.e.
D(Z_{n})\to 0
. Many more results on entropic CLTs can be found in
[
36
]
. Moreover, in
[
13
]
, an Edgeworth expansion was used to determine the rates of convergence in the entropic CLT for the classical statistic.
We prove such type of result for the self-normalized sum.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, has a bounded density and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 3
. Then

D(T_{n})=\frac{c_{2}}{n^{2}}+\dots+\frac{c_{\lfloor(m-2)/2\rfloor}}{n^{\lfloor(m-2)/2\rfloor}}+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big((n\log n)^{-(m-2)/2}\,\log n\big),

where

\displaystyle c_{l}=\sum_{k=2}^{2l}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\sum\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{r_{1}}(x)\dots q_{r_{k}}(x)}{\phi(x)^{k-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad l\in\mathbb{N},

with the inner sum running over all positive integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
such that
r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}=2l
.

Theorem

1.3
also holds under the weaker but more technical conditions of
Theorem

4.1
, see
Theorem

5.1
.

Remark 1.4
.

Comparing the relative entropy of
T_{n}
and
Z_{n}
under the conditions of
Theorem

1.3
with
m=6
, we obtain

\displaystyle D(T_{n})

\displaystyle=n^{-2}\tfrac{1}{12}\mu_{4}^{2}+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big((n\log n)^{-2}\,\log n\big)

and (from
[
13
]
)

\displaystyle D(Z_{n})=n^{-2}\tfrac{1}{48}(\mu_{4}-3)^{2}+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big((n\log n)^{-2}\big).

Note that under these conditions,
\mu_{4}^{2}/12>(\mu_{4}-3)^{2}/48
. The relative entropies are always of the same magnitude and particularly close if
\mu_{4}
is close to 1. The limit case
\mu_{4}=1
is excluded because
X_{1}
has a density.

Our final goal is prove a bound on the total variation. On the linear space of finite signed measures on
(\mathbb{R},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}))
, the total variation norm is defined as

\Arrowvert\mu\Arrowvert_{TV}=\sup_{B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})}\Big(\arrowvert\mu(B)\arrowvert+\arrowvert\mu(B^{c})\arrowvert\Big)

To circumvent unnecessary notational complexity, we identify finite (signed) measures with their corresponding cumulative distribution functions.
If
F_{P}
and
F_{Q}
have densities, bounding their distance in total variation is equivalent to bounding the
L^{1}
norm of the difference of the corresponding densities

\|F_{n}-\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}\|_{\textup{TV}}=\|f_{n}-\phi^{q}_{m,n}\|_{L^{1}}.

(1.8)

Assume that the conditions of
Theorem

1.2
are satisfied, in particular
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 3
. Then the second statement of
Theorem

1.2
applied with
m-1
implies

\displaystyle\|f_{n}-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}\|_{L^{p}}^{p}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|(1+|x|)^{-(m-1)}r_{n}\big|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq c\,r_{n}^{p}

for
p\in[1,\infty)
and an absolute constant
c>0
. Here,
r_{n}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}\,\log n\big)
and thus

\|f_{n}-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}\|_{L^{p}}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}\log n\big).

(1.9)

The following theorem shows that we can achieve a stronger order of convergence by methods that are particularly suitable for optimizing the
TV
distance.

Theorem 1.5
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, has a bounded density and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 3
. Then

\|F_{n}-\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}\|_{\textup{TV}}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}\big).

Theorem

1.5
also holds under the weaker but more technical conditions of
Theorem

4.1
, see
Theorem

6.1
.

The remainder of this article is organized as follows. In
Section

2
, we introduce further notation and the formal Edgeworth expansion for self-normalized sums. The CLT-type result (
Theorem

1.1
) is proven in
Section

3
.
Section

4
provides more general LLTs that lead to
Theorem

1.2
. In
Sections

5
and
6
, the prior results are used to attain the entropic CLT (
Theorem

1.3
) and the CLT in total variation distance (
Theorem

1.5
), respectively.

2
Notation and preliminaries

The notation
\varphi
is used for the characteristic functions of random variables or the Fourier transforms of functions. Their dependence is indicated by a subscript, e.g.
\varphi_{T_{n}}
or
\varphi_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}
, while
\varphi_{X_{j}}
is abbreviated as
\varphi_{j}
. For
k\geq 0
,
c(k)
represents different positive constants in different (or even in the same) formulas, only depending on
k
and the distribution of
X_{1}
.
\lceil x\rceil
denotes the smallest integer larger than or equal to
x
.

If a sum
\sum
, product
\prod
or a maximum
\max
does not have an index, it is always meant to run over
j=1,\dots,n
. The sum
\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}
is carried out over all non-negative integer solutions
(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{r})
of the equalities
k_{1}+2k_{2}+\dots+rk_{r}=r
and
k_{1}+k_{2}+\dots+k_{r}=u
. In many occurrences, only the variable
r
is fixed which will be denoted by
\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}
. Then the
k_{i}
only have to satisfy the equation
k_{1}+2k_{2}+\dots+rk_{r}=r
and
u=u(k_{\cdot})
is defined by
u(k_{\cdot})=k_{1}+k_{2}+\dots+k_{r}
. In some situations only the variable
u
is fixed. In this case we need information on the maximal index of
k_{\cdot}
. This is denoted by
\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,t)}
meaning the summation over all non-negative integer solutions
(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{t})
of the equation
k_{1}+k_{2}+\dots+k_{t}=u
and
r=r(k_{\cdot})
is defined by
r(k_{\cdot})=k_{1}+2k_{2}+\dots+tk_{t}
. Clearly
u\leq r
holds.

We set

M_{n}=\max|X_{j}|\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{n}=V_{n}/M_{n}.

As
V_{n}=0
and
M_{n}=0
are equivalent, we define
B_{n}=1
in this case, whence
1\leq B_{n}\leq\sqrt{n}
is always satisfied. For
l\geq 0
the
l
-th cumulant of
X_{1}
will be denoted by
\kappa_{l}
and is defined by

\kappa_{l}=i^{-l}{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\log\varphi_{1}(t)\Big|_{t=0}\,.

(2.1)

The conditional expected value with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{n}
is shortened as
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\ \cdot\ ]=\mathbb{E}[\ \cdot\ |\mathcal{F}_{n}]
. Correspondingly, the
\sim
-notation is extended to
\mathbb{P},\mu,\kappa,F,f,p,P,U,\varphi
for describing the variants conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. We define

\widetilde{L}_{k,n}=V_{n}^{-k}\sum|X_{j}|^{k}

for
k\geq 2
integer. Clearly
V_{n}=0
and
\sum|X_{j}|^{k}=0
are equivalent. In this case, we define
\widetilde{L}_{k,n}=0
. Note that for all
k\geq 2
,

\widetilde{L}_{k,n}=V_{n}^{-k}\sum|X_{j}|^{k}\leq V_{n}^{-2}\,\frac{M_{n}^{k-2}}{V_{n}^{k-2}}\,\sum|X_{j}|^{2}\leq V_{n}^{-2}\,\sum|X_{j}|^{2}=1.

(2.2)

When we state that the sequence
(A_{n})
increases (or decreases, respectively) in polynomial order, we mean that there exist
a,b>0
(or
a,b<0
, respectively) such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Big|\frac{n^{a}}{A_{n}}\Big|<\infty\quad\text{and}\quad\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Big|\frac{A_{n}}{n^{b}}\Big|<\infty.

When writing (probability) densities, we exclusively mean Lebesgue densities. Densities with respect to the counting measure will be denoted as probability mass functions. When mentioning a density (or a function in general), we always refer to the continuous version of it in case it exists.

2.1
Edgeworth expansions for self-normalized sums

In this subsection, we introduce the Edgeworth expansion for
T_{n}
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and establish a connection to
1.1
and
1.5
. In particular, the explicit form of the approximating polynomials is derived for later purposes. To this end, we assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric such that
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(X_{j}=\pm|X_{j}|)=1/2
,
j=1,\dots,n
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\widetilde{\mu}_{k,j}=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[X_{j}^{k}]
the
k
-th conditional moment of
X_{j}
. Accordingly, we extend the notation to
\widetilde{\kappa}_{k,j}
.
Note that

\widetilde{\kappa}_{2k-1,j}=\widetilde{\mu}_{2k-1,j}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{\mu}_{2k,j}=|X_{j}|^{2k}.

(2.3)

Although not necessary, we state the absolute value for even powers of
X_{j}
to point out that we operate in the conditional setting where the absolute values are deterministic. By explicitly computing the values of the Bell polynomials, the
k
-th cumulant can be expressed in terms of moments up to degree
k

which yields

\begin{split}\widetilde{\kappa}_{2r,j}=|X_{j}|^{2r}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}(-1)^{u(k_{\cdot})-1}(u(k_{\cdot})-1)!(2r)!\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!(2l)!^{k_{l}}}\end{split}

(2.4)

for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
because all summands with one or more
k_{2l-1}>0
vanish due to
2.3
. In particular,

\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,j}=|X_{j}|^{2},\qquad\widetilde{\kappa}_{4,j}=-2|X_{j}|^{4},\qquad\widetilde{\kappa}_{6,j}=16|X_{j}|^{6}.

Below, we outline the characteristics of the Edgeworth expansion of
T_{n}
, conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Clearly, the classical Edgeworth expansion (see e.g.
[
38
, Section VI.1]
) needs to be adjusted in terms of approximating polynomials and functions. In particular, for
l,r,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{l,n}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum\widetilde{\mu}_{2,j}\Big)^{-l/2}\sum\widetilde{\kappa}_{l,j}=V_{n}^{-l}\sum\widetilde{\kappa}_{l,j}\quad(\widetilde{\lambda}_{l,n}=0\text{ if }V_{n}=0),

(2.5)

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)

\displaystyle=\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}(it)^{r+2u(k_{\cdot})}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}},

(2.6)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)

\displaystyle=e^{-t^{2}/2}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)\Big).

(2.7)

Next, the Edgeworth expansion for the conditional distribution function of
T_{n}
is

\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)=\Phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{P}_{r,n}(x),

(2.8)

where

\widetilde{P}_{r,n}(x)=-\phi(x)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}H_{r+2u(k_{\cdot})-1}(x)\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}

(2.9)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
In
2.6
and
2.9
, all summands with one or more
k_{2l-1}>0
vanish due to
2.3
. For uneven indices the polynomials only consist of such summands and therefore
\widetilde{U}_{2r-1,n}=\widetilde{P}_{2r-1,n}=0
for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
. The even polynomials
\widetilde{P}_{2r,n}
reduce to

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{2r,n}(x)

\displaystyle=-\phi(x)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}H_{2r+2u(k_{\cdot})-1}(x)\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{2l+2,n}}{(2l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{2,n}(x)

\displaystyle=-\phi(x)H_{3}(x)\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{4,n}}{4!},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{4,n}(x)

\displaystyle=-\phi(x)\Big(H_{7}(x)\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{4,n}}{4!}\Big)^{2}+H_{5}(x)\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{6,n}}{6!}\Big).

Taking expectations of
\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}
and
\widetilde{P}_{r,n}
respectively returns us to the unconditional setting. In
Remark

A.1
, the expectation of the
\widetilde{\lambda}_{l,n}
appearing in
\widetilde{P}_{2,n}
and
\widetilde{P}_{4,n}
is evaluated – the only source of randomness in
2.1
. For now, let all moments be finite for clear illustration. The evaluation results in

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{4,n}}{4!}\bigg]&=n^{-1}\big(-\tfrac{1}{12}\big)\mu_{4}+n^{-2}\tfrac{1}{12}\big(2\mu_{6}+\mu_{4}-3\mu_{4}^{2}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-3}\big),\\
\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{6,n}}{6!}\bigg]&=n^{-2}\tfrac{1}{45}\mu_{6}+n^{-3}\big(-\tfrac{1}{45}\big)(3\mu_{8}+3\mu_{6}-6\mu_{4}\mu_{6})+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-4}\big),\\
\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{4,n}}{4!}\Big)^{2}\bigg]&=n^{-2}\tfrac{1}{288}\mu_{4}^{2}+n^{-3}\big(-\tfrac{1}{288}\big)(8\mu_{6}\mu_{4}-7\mu_{4}^{2}-\mu_{8})+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-4}\big).\end{split}

(2.11)

As the expected value of
\widetilde{\lambda}_{l,n}
(and its powers) admit an expansion in powers of
n^{-1}
, it becomes evident that
\mathbb{E}\big[\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}\big]
also admits an expansion in powers of
n^{-1}
. However, we will keep using the common notation of an expansion in powers of
n^{-1/2}
. For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, the approximating polynomial
Q_{r}
is thus defined as the coefficient of
n^{-r/2}
in the expansion of

\mathbb{E}\big[\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\big]=\Phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}Q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\big)

(2.12)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. As a result, we may define the approximating functions for the distribution function of
T_{n}
by
1.1
.

Combining
2.1
,
2.12
and
2.11
, it is apparent that the first polynomials have the form
1.2
. As
\widetilde{P}_{2r-1,n}=0
and the expected value of the relevant
\widetilde{\lambda}
and its powers do not provide terms of orders
n^{-(2r-1)/2}
for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
, also
Q_{2r-1}=0
for all
r\in\mathbb{N}
.
The approximating polynomials
Q_{r}
can be directly calculated by Chung’s method
[
20
,
23
]
, by the
\delta
-method or by the smooth function model which are both explained in detail in
[
30
, Chapter 2]
.

The Edgeworth expansion can also be applied to the density of a normalized sum (see e.g.
[
8
, Chapter 4]
,
[
12
]
,
[
30
, Section 2.8]
,
[
38
, Section VII.3]
). Concerning this expansion for the density, we state the suitable density-related functions for our setting. By differentiating
2.9
, for
r\in\mathbb{N}
we get the approximating polynomials for the conditional density function

\widetilde{p}_{r,n}(x)=\phi(x)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}H_{r+2u(k_{\cdot})}(x)\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}},

(2.13)

(where clearly
\widetilde{p}_{2r-1,n}=0
) and thus differentiation
2.8
yields the Edgeworth expansion for the density of
T_{n}

\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)=\phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{p}_{r,n}(x)

(2.14)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
As above, for
r\in\mathbb{N}
we define the approximating polynomials
q_{r}
as the coefficient of
n^{-r/2}
in the expansion of

\mathbb{E}\big[\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\big]=\phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\big)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and the approximating functions for the density of
T_{n}
by
1.5
. In consequence, the first approximating polynomials
q_{r}
have the form
1.6
. Note

\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)={\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}}\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad q_{r}(x)={\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}}Q_{r}(x).

As we will not assume all moments to be finite, the remainder terms have to be specified. In view of the non-uniform bounds, their dependence on the argument
x
is crucial.

Proposition 2.1
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{{s}}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
. Then for
m=\lfloor{s}\rfloor
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,\exp(x^{2}/4)\,\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\big]-\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)\big|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)

(2.15)

and

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,\exp(x^{2}/4)\,\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\big]-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)\big|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

(2.16)

Although this result could have been expected, its proof (see
Section

A.1
) is quite elaborate. Since the expectation of
\widetilde{\lambda}_{l,n}
cannot be explicitly evaluated, we study the expectation of its Taylor expansion around
n^{-l/2}\sum\widetilde{\kappa}_{l,j}
. Here, powers of
X_{1}
that are higher than
s
arise. As we need a bound in order of
n
while controlling the powers of
X_{1}
, tight bounds are developed on the critical summands. We show that by definition (see
2.8
and
2.9
),
\mathbb{E}\big[\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\big]
possesses a similar expansion and similar bounds that result in
Proposition

2.1
when combined with
2.12
.
Finally, we need some bounds on the approximation functions.

Remark 2.2
.

All summands in
2.6
with
k_{1}>0
are 0 as
\widetilde{\lambda}_{3,n}=0
. Therefore, in all non-zero summands
k_{1}=0
and thus
u(k_{\cdot})\leq r/2
. Additionally, note that the only
n
-dependence in the following expressions is in
\widetilde{\lambda}_{l,n}
, which satisfies
|\widetilde{\lambda}_{l,n}|\leq c(l)
for all
l=0,\dots,m
due to
2.2
. Then from
2.7
,

\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}}(t)\Big|=\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\Big(e^{-t^{2}/2}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)\Big)\Big)\Big|\leq c(m)e^{-t^{2}/2}\big(1+|t|^{2m-4+l}\big)

(2.17)

for
l=0,\dots,m
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Equivalently, from
2.9
,

\big|\widetilde{P}_{r,n}(x)\big|\leq c(m)\phi(x)(1+|x|^{2r-1})\leq c(m)

(2.18)

and from
2.13
,

\big|\widetilde{p}_{r,n}(x)\big|\leq c(m)\phi(x)(1+|x|^{2r})\leq c(m)

(2.19)

for
r=0,\dots,m
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Here, the constants
c(m)
can be chosen uniformly for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

3
Non-uniform bounds for Edgeworth expansions in the central limit theorem for self-normalized sums

Recall
\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)=\Phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}Q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}
from
1.1
. For convenience, we restate
Theorem

1.1
which is the goal of this section:

Theorem
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{s}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
. Then for
m=\lfloor{s}\rfloor
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|F_{n}(x)-\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+2m)/2}\big).

For
s\in[2,4)
,
\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}
reduces to
\Phi
. Here, the non-uniform Berry–Esseen bounds in
[
33
,
40
]
allow for better bounds in view of the argument
x
than
Theorem

1.1
.
Without the factor
(1+|x|)^{m}
, this result has been derived in
[
28
]
, but only for integer
{s}
. For
Z_{n}
, non-uniform bound in the spirit of ours can be found in
[
38
, Theorem 2, Chapter VI]
, for instance.

Structure of the proof

First, we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and derive a result in the conditional setting (
Proposition

3.4
) by showing a bound on the characteristic functions and their derivatives (
Proposition

3.2
) and using an extension of the Fourier inversion formula. Afterwards we return to the unconditional setting by taking the expectation. On the left-hand side, we employ
Proposition

2.1
to move from
\mathbb{E}\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}
to
\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}
while on the right-hand side, we evaluate the expectation of multiple rather involved random remainder terms (
Propositions

3.5
,
3.8
,
3.9
and
3.6
), proving
Theorem

1.1
.
However, when returning to the unconditional setting by taking expectations, we face similar problems as
[
20
,
28
]
including the expectation of the absolute value of the conditional characteristic function. Same as our precursors, we solve this problem by demanding for non-singularity.

Remark 3.1

(Non-singularity)
.

The condition of the distribution of
X_{1}
being non-singular with respect to the Lebesgue measure is sometimes also denoted as the distribution of
X_{1}
having a non-zero absolutely continuous component.
For
Z_{n}
the analogue condition is Cramér’s condition
1.4
which we compare to non-singularity now.

By the Lebesgue decomposition, for every distribution function there exists a unique decomposition

F=p_{ac}F_{ac}+p_{d}F_{d}+p_{sc}F_{sc},

(3.1)

with
p_{ac}+p_{d}+p_{sc}=1
,
p_{\cdot}\geq 0
where
F_{\cdot}
are the distribution functions of an absolutely continuous distribution (
F_{ac}
), a discrete distribution (
F_{d}
) and a singular continuous distribution (
F_{sc}
) respectively. This corresponding decomposition holds for the characteristic function.

By the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma
[
8
, Theorem 4.1 (iii)]
,
\limsup_{|t|\to\infty}|\varphi_{1,ac}(t)]|=0
and by
[
42
]
,
\limsup_{|t|\to\infty}|\varphi_{1,d}(t)]|=1
and
\limsup_{|t|\to\infty}|\varphi_{1,sc}(t)]|\in[0,1]
(the
\limsup
can in fact take all values in the interval).
That is why non-singularity implies
1.4
. If
\limsup_{|t|\to\infty}|\varphi_{1,sc}(t)]|=1
(for examples see
[
22
, p. 164]
or
[
42
]
) or
p_{sc}=0
, non-singularity and
1.4
are equivalent and both imply that the distribution of
X_{1}
is not purely discrete.

The reason for our need for the stronger condition of non-singularity is that we need a variant of Cramér’s condition including the expectation of the absolute value of the conditional characteristic function in
3.41
in the proof of
Proposition

3.5
. This requires the stronger assumption of non-singularity due to the absolute value within the expectation.

For discrete lattice distributions (which are neither non-singular nor satisfy
1.4
), Edgeworth expansions for CLT and LLT of
Z_{n}
can be found in
[
8
, Chapter 5]
. For
T_{n}
, the recent reference
[
27
]
presents limit theorems and interesting occurring phenomena.

3.1
The conditional setting

Recall the definitions
M_{n}=\max|X_{j}|
,
B_{n}=V_{n}/M_{n}
,
\widetilde{L}_{k,n}=V_{n}^{-k}\sum|X_{j}|^{k}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)=e^{-t^{2}/2}\big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)\big)
,
where

\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)=\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}(it)^{r+2u(k_{\cdot})}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}

from
2.6
and
2.7
and the sum
\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}
is introduced in
Section

2
.

The following proposition examines differences of derivatives of the Fourier–Stieltjes transforms of
\widetilde{F}_{n}
and
\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}
around 0. Although versions of this proposition are a key part of every CLT or LLT including an Edgeworth expansion, the crucial point here is that the random variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
are discrete and furthermore not identically distributed any longer.

Proposition 3.2
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric,
V_{n}^{2}>0
and
m\geq 2
is an integer. Then

\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\Big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\Big)\Big|\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}e^{-t^{2}/6}\big(|t|^{m+1-k}+|t|^{3m-1+k}\big)

holds for
k=0,\dots,m
in the interval
|t|<B_{n}
.

Although there are many significant differences, the structure of our proof has its origins in the proof of
[
38
, Lemma 4, Chapter VI]
. Prior to proving the proposition, we cite Lemma 2 from
[
38
, Chapter VI]
in the conditional case. It gives an upper bound on
\widetilde{L}_{a,n}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be independent random variables and
\mathbb{E}X_{j}=0,\mathbb{E}X_{j}^{2}<\infty
for all
j=1,\dots,n
. If
3\leq a\leq b
, then

\widetilde{L}_{a,n}^{1/(a-2)}\leq\widetilde{L}_{b,n}^{1/(b-2)}.

X_{j}
has the conditional characteristic function

\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(t)=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}[\exp(itX_{j})]=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\exp(it|X_{j}|)+\exp(-it|X_{j}|)\Big)=\cos(t|X_{j}|)

for
j=1,\dots,n
. Clearly
\cos(t|X_{j}|)=\cos(tX_{j})
, but we keep the first notation to highlight that we are operating in the conditional setting where
|X_{j}|
is deterministic. By conditional independence we can write for the conditional Fourier transform of
T_{n}

\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)=\prod\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\big[\exp(itV_{n}^{-1}X_{j})\big]=\prod\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tV_{n}^{-1})=\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big).

(3.2)

For applying the logarithm later, we need to bound
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tV_{n}^{-1})=\cos(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|)
away from 0 for all
j=1,\dots,n
. So we restrict ourselves on the interval
|t|<B_{n}
throughout the rest of the proof. Thus,

\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tV_{n}^{-1})=\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\geq\cos(1)>\tfrac{1}{2}

(3.3)

for all
j=1,\dots,n
.

Since
X_{j}
are symmetric,
\sum\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,j}=0
and
\sum\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,j}=V_{n}^{2}
. Recall
\widetilde{\lambda}_{l,n}=V_{n}^{-l}\sum\widetilde{\kappa}_{l,j}
from
2.5
. By taking the logarithm, we thus get (see
2.1
)

\displaystyle\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tV_{n}^{-1})=\sum\sum_{l=1}^{m}\widetilde{\kappa}_{l,j}\frac{(itV_{n}^{-1})^{l}}{l!}+\mathcal{O}(t^{m+1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{m-2}\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}(it)^{l+2}-\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(t^{m+1}).

The series expansion is valid as all conditional cumulants are finite. To eliminate the normal part (which is
-t^{2}/2
), we define

v_{n}(t,z):=\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(tz)=\sum_{l=1}^{m-2}\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}(it)^{l+2}z^{l}+\mathcal{O}(t^{m+1},z^{m-1})

(3.4)

for
0<z\leq 1
.
Now we expand
e^{v_{n}(t,z)}
as a series in
z
(with fixed
t
)

e^{v_{n}(t,z)}=1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)z^{r}+\mathcal{O}(z^{m-1})

such that

\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(tz)^{1/z^{2}}=\exp\Big(-\frac{t^{2}}{2}+v_{n}(t,z)\Big)=e^{-t^{2}/2}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)z^{r}+R_{n}(t,z)\Big).

(3.5)

Recalling
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)=e^{-t^{2}/2}\big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)\big)
, we now have to show that
R_{n}(t,z)
and its derivatives are small for
z\to 1
.

First, we examine (see e.g.
[
22
, Theorem 3.3.18]
)

\begin{split}\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|$\mbox{}\hfil\quad\\
&=\Big|\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big(izV_{n}^{-1}X_{j}\big)^{l}\exp(itzV_{n}^{-1}X_{j})\Big]\Big|\\
&\leq\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\Big[z^{l}V_{n}^{-l}|X_{j}|^{l}\Big]=z^{l}V_{n}^{-l}|X_{j}|^{l}\end{split}

(3.6)

for
j=1,\dots,n
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
.

According to the chain rule (see e.g.
[
38
, Lemma 1, Chapter VI]
), we derive

\begin{split}&\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|\\
&\quad=\Big|r!\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}(-1)^{u(k_{\cdot})-1}(u(k_{\cdot})-1)!\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})^{-u(k_{\cdot})}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{1}{l!}{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\widetilde{\varphi}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big)^{k_{l}}\Big|\\
&\quad\leq c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\big(z^{l}V_{n}^{-l}|X_{j}|^{l}\big)^{k_{l}}=c(r)z^{r}V_{n}^{-r}|X_{j}|^{r}\end{split}

(3.7)

for
j=1,\dots,n
and
r=1,\dots,m+1
, where we used
3.3
and
3.6
.

By Taylor expansion of
v_{n}(t,z)
(at the point
0
, with an intermediate point
u\in\mathbb{R}
between
0
and
t
, depending on
v_{n}(\cdot,z)
and
t
) using
2.1
, we get

\displaystyle v_{n}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})

\displaystyle=v_{n}(0,z)

\displaystyle\quad+t\Big(0+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum zV_{n}^{-1}\widetilde{\kappa}_{1,j}\,i\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{t^{2}}{2}\Big(1+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum(zV_{n}^{-1})^{2}\widetilde{\kappa}_{2,j}\,i^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{t^{3}}{6z^{2}}\sum{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}t^{3}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|_{t=u}

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{3}}{6z^{2}}\sum{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}t^{3}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|_{t=u}\,.

Now
3.7
implies

|v_{n}(t,z)|\leq\frac{|t|^{3}}{6z^{2}}\sum c(3)z^{3}V_{n}^{-3}|X_{j}|^{3}\leq c(3)|t|^{3}V_{n}^{-3}\sum|X_{j}|^{3}=c(3)|t|^{3}\widetilde{L}_{3,n}.

(3.8)

By a Taylor expansion of
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}v_{n}(t,z)
(at the point
0
, with an intermediate point
u\in\mathbb{R}
between
0
and
t
and
u
depending on
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}v_{n}(\cdot,z)
and
t
), we get in the same manner

\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}v_{n}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{t^{2}}{2z^{2}}\sum{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}}{\mathrm{d}t^{3}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|_{t=u}

which leads to

\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}}v_{n}(t,z)\Big|\leq\frac{t^{2}}{2z^{2}}\sum c(3)z^{3}V_{n}^{-3}|X_{j}|^{3}\leq c(3)|t|^{2}V_{n}^{-3}\sum|X_{j}|^{3}=c(3)|t|^{2}\widetilde{L}_{3,n}

(3.9)

by
3.7
. Similarly,

\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}t^{2}}}v_{n}(t,z)\Big|\leq c(3)|t|V_{n}^{-3}\sum|X_{j}|^{3}=c(3)|t|\widetilde{L}_{3,n}.

(3.10)

For
l=3,\dots,m
, the definition of
v_{n}(t,z)
and
3.7
directly give

\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}v_{n}(t,z)\Big|\leq\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum c(l)z^{l}V_{n}^{-l}|X_{j}|^{l}=c(l)\widetilde{L}_{l,n}\,z^{l-2}\leq c(l)\widetilde{L}_{l,n}.

(3.11)

The necessity of bounding the next term will become evident in
3.15
,
3.16
,
3.17
and
3.18
. For
{1\leq r\leq m\leq h}
, the chain rule for powers of functions (see for example
[
38
, Lemma 3, Chapter VI]
) and our derived estimates
3.8
,
3.9
,
3.10
and
3.11
gives

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{h!}{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r}}}v_{n}^{h}(t,z)\Big|=\Big|\frac{r!\cdot h!}{h!}\sum_{u=1}^{r\land h}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}\frac{v_{n}^{h-u}(t,z)}{(h-u)!}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{1}{l!}{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}v_{n}(t,z)\Big)^{k_{l}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\sum_{u=1}^{r}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}\frac{|v_{n}(t,z)|^{h-m}}{(h-m)!}|v_{n}(t,z)|^{m-u}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}v_{n}(t,z)\Big|^{k_{l}}

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\frac{|v_{n}(t,z)|^{h-m}}{(h-m)!}\sum_{u=1}^{r}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}\big(|t|^{3}\widetilde{L}_{3,n}\big)^{m-u}\big(|t|^{2}\widetilde{L}_{3,n}\big)^{k_{1}}\big(|t|\widetilde{L}_{3,n}\big)^{k_{2}}\prod_{l=3}^{r}\widetilde{L}_{l,n}^{k_{l}}

\displaystyle=c(m)\frac{|v_{n}(t,z)|^{h-m}}{(h-m)!}\sum_{u=1}^{r}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}|t|^{3m-3u+2k_{1}+k_{2}}\widetilde{L}_{3,n}^{m-u+k_{1}+k_{2}}\prod_{l=3}^{r}\widetilde{L}_{l,n}^{k_{l}}.

Now,
Lemma

3.3
(with
b=m+1
) implies that this is bounded by

\displaystyle c(m)

\displaystyle\frac{|v_{n}(t,z)|^{h-m}}{(h-m)!}\sum_{u=1}^{r}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}|t|^{3m-3u+2k_{1}+k_{2}}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{(m-u+k_{1}+k_{2})/(m-1)}\prod_{l=3}^{r}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{(l-2)k_{l}/(m-1)}

\displaystyle=c(m)\frac{|v_{n}(t,z)|^{h-m}}{(h-m)!}\sum_{u=1}^{r}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}|t|^{3m-3u+2k_{1}+k_{2}}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{(m-3u+2k_{1}+k_{2}+r)/(m-1)}

\displaystyle=c(m)\frac{|v_{n}(t,z)|^{h-m}}{(h-m)!}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}|t|^{3m-r-1}\sum_{u=1}^{r}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}\big(|t|\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{1/(m-1)}\big)^{1-3u+2k_{1}+k_{2}+r},

where the last exponent is greater than 0 due to

2k_{1}+k_{2}+r=3k_{1}+3k_{2}+3k_{3}+4k_{4}+\dots+rk_{r}\geq 3(k_{1}+k_{2}+\dots+k_{r})=3u.

For
k\geq 3
, we can bound

|t|^{k-2}\widetilde{L}_{k,n}\leq B_{n}^{k-2}\widetilde{L}_{k,n}=\frac{V_{n}^{k-2}\sum|X_{j}|^{k}}{V_{n}^{k}M_{n}^{k-2}}\leq\frac{\sum|X_{j}|^{2}}{V_{n}^{2}}=1

(3.12)

and thus

|t|\widetilde{L}_{k,n}^{1/(k-2)}\leq 1

(3.13)

which in case of
k=m+1
leads to

\Big|\frac{1}{h!}{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r}}}v_{n}^{h}(t,z)\Big|\leq c(m)\frac{|v_{n}(t,z)|^{h-m}}{(h-m)!}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}|t|^{3m-r-1}

(3.14)

for
1\leq r\leq m\leq h
.

We look back at
3.5
and define

R_{n}(t,z)=R_{n,1}(t,z)+R_{n,2}(t,z),

(3.15)

where

\displaystyle R_{n,1}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=0}^{m-1}\frac{v_{n}^{u}(t,z)}{u!}-\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)z^{r}\Big),

(3.16)

\displaystyle R_{n,2}(t,z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=m}^{\infty}\frac{v_{n}^{u}(t,z)}{u!}.

(3.17)

By
3.14
, this implies (with
h=u
and
r=a
)

\begin{split}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}}R_{n,2}(t,z)\Big|&\leq\sum_{u=m}^{\infty}\Big|\frac{1}{u!}{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}}v_{n}^{u}(t,z)\Big|\\
&\leq\sum_{u=m}^{\infty}c(m)\frac{|v_{n}(t,z)|^{u-m}}{(u-m)!}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}|t|^{3m-a-1}\\
&=c(m)e^{|v_{n}(t,z)|}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}|t|^{3m-a-1}\end{split}

(3.18)

for all
a=1,\dots,m
. By
3.8
,
3.12
and
Lemma

3.3
,

\begin{split}\Big|R_{n,2}(t,z)\Big|&\leq\sum_{u=m}^{\infty}\Big|\frac{1}{u!}v_{n}^{u}(t,z)\Big|\\
&\leq e^{|v_{n}(t,z)|}|v_{n}(t,z)|^{m}\\
&\leq c(m)e^{|v_{n}(t,z)|}\widetilde{L}_{3,n}^{m-1}|t|^{3m-1}\\
&\leq c(m)e^{|v_{n}(t,z)|}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}|t|^{3m-1},\end{split}

(3.19)

so
3.18
also holds for
a=0
.

Let
x>-1
. As

{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}}\big(\log(1+x)-(x-x^{2})\big)=\frac{1}{1+x}-1+2x

is less than or equal to 0 for
x\in[-1/2,0]
and greater than or equal to 0 for
x\geq 0
, the function
x\mapsto\log(1+x)-(x-x^{2})
is decreasing for
x\in[-1/2,0]
and increasing for
x\geq 0
. Additionally, it is continuous and
\log(1+0)-(0-0^{2})=0
. Hence,
\log(1+x)-(x-x^{2})\geq 0
for
x\geq-1/2
and therefore
\log(1+x)\geq x-x^{2}
for
x\geq-1/2
.
Additionally
\log(1+x)\leq x
. Thus, for every
x\geq-1/2
there exists a
\vartheta
(depending on
x
) with
0\leq\vartheta\leq 1
such that
\log(1+x)=x-\vartheta x^{2}
.
By
3.3
,
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})>1/2
for all
j=1,\dots,n
and thus by inserting
x=\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})-1
into the equation above,

\displaystyle|v_{n}(t,z)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\Big(\big(\cos(tzV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|)-1\big)+\vartheta_{j}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})-1\big)^{2}\Big)\Big|

for some
\vartheta_{j}
(depending on
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})
), where
|\vartheta_{j}|\leq 1
for
j=1,\dots,n
. Additionally, there exists
\vartheta^{\prime}
(depending on
x
) with the properties
\cos(x)=1-x^{2}/2+\vartheta^{\prime}x^{4}/(4!)
and
{0\leq\vartheta^{\prime}\leq 1}
and
|\cos(x)-1|\leq x^{2}/2
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
. By
3.12
, we deduce

\displaystyle|v_{n}(t,z)|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\Big(\big(\cos(tzV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|)-1\big)+\vartheta_{j}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})-1\big)^{2}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\Big(\big(\vartheta_{j}^{\prime}\tfrac{(tzV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|)^{4}}{24}\big)+\vartheta_{j}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})-1\big)^{2}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t^{4}z^{2}}{24\,V_{n}^{4}}\sum|X_{j}|^{4}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\big|\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})-1\big|^{2}

(3.20)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t^{4}z^{2}}{24}\widetilde{L}_{4,n}+\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\sum\big|\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})-1\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t^{2}z^{2}}{24}(t^{2}\widetilde{L}_{4,n})+\frac{1}{2z^{2}}\sum\frac{(tzV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|)^{2}}{2}\leq\frac{t^{2}}{3}

for some
\vartheta_{j}^{\prime}
(depending on
tzV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|
), where
0\leq\vartheta_{j}^{\prime}\leq 1
for
j=1,\dots,n
.

Putting together
3.18
,
3.19
and
3.20
implies

\begin{split}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}}R_{n,2}(t,z)\Big|\leq c(m)e^{|v_{n}(t,z)|}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}|t|^{3m-a-1}\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}|t|^{3m-a-1}e^{t^{2}/3}\end{split}

(3.21)

for all
a=0,\dots,m
.

Now to
R_{n,1}
. By
3.4
, we have

v_{n}(t,z)=\sum_{k=1}^{m-2}\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k+2,n}}{(k+2)!}(it)^{k+2}z^{k}+r_{n}(t,z)z^{m-1},

(3.22)

where
r_{n}
is the remainder of a Taylor expansion (at the point
0
, with intermediate point
u\in\mathbb{R}
between
0
and
t
and
u
depending on
v_{n}(\cdot,z)
and
t
)

r_{n}(t,z)=\frac{t^{m+1}}{(m+1)!z^{m-1}}\;{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m+1}}{\mathrm{d}t^{m+1}}}v_{n}(t,z)\Big|_{t=u}.

For
l=0,\dots,m
and
j=1,\dots,n
, by Taylor expansion of
{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})
(at
0
, with intermediate point
u_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
between
0
and
t
, depending on
{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(\cdot\,zV_{n}^{-1})
and
t
) and
2.1
,

\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-l}\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\;{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l+k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l+k}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|_{t=0}+\frac{t^{m-l+1}}{(m-l+1)!}\;{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m+1}}{\mathrm{d}t^{m+1}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|_{t=u_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=l}^{m}\frac{\widetilde{\kappa}_{k,j}}{V_{n}^{k}}z^{k}i^{k}\frac{t^{k-l}}{(k-l)!}+\frac{t^{m-l+1}}{(m-l+1)!}\;{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m+1}}{\mathrm{d}t^{m+1}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|_{t=u_{j}}

for some
0\leq u_{j}\leq t
.
For
l=0,\dots,m
, differentiating
3.22
and using
3.7
implies

\displaystyle\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}r_{n}(t,z)\Big|

\displaystyle=z^{-(m-1)}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\Big(\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big)-{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\sum_{k=1}^{m-2}\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k+2,n}}{(k+2)!}(it)^{k+2}z^{k}\Big|

\displaystyle=z^{-(m-1)}\Big|\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\frac{t^{2}}{2}\Big)+\Big(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big)-\sum_{k=3\vee l}^{m}\widetilde{\lambda}_{k,n}i^{k}\frac{t^{k-l}}{(k-l)!}z^{k-2}\Big|

\displaystyle=z^{-(m-1)}\bigg|\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\frac{t^{2}}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\Big(\sum_{k=l}^{m}\frac{\widetilde{\kappa}_{k,j}}{V_{n}^{k}}z^{k}i^{k}\frac{t^{k-l}}{(k-l)!}+\Big(\frac{t^{m-l+1}}{(m-l+1)!}\;{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m+1}}{\mathrm{d}t^{m+1}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|_{t=u_{j}}\Big)\Big)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{k=l}^{m}\widetilde{\lambda}_{k,n}i^{k}\frac{t^{k-l}}{(k-l)!}z^{k-2}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{l\leq 2\}}\widetilde{\lambda}_{2,n}i^{2}\frac{t^{2-l}}{(2-l)!}\,\bigg|

\displaystyle=z^{-(m-1)}\Big|\frac{1}{z^{2}}\sum\frac{t^{m-l+1}}{(m-l+1)!}\;{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m+1}}{\mathrm{d}t^{m+1}}}\log\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(tzV_{n}^{-1})\Big|_{t=u_{j}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq z^{-(m+1)}\frac{|t|^{m-l+1}}{(m-l+1)!}\sum c(m)z^{m+1}V_{n}^{-(m+1)}|X_{j}|^{m+1}

\displaystyle=c(m)|t|^{m-l+1}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}.

For
r,k=0,\dots,m
by chain rule, this leads to

\displaystyle\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}t^{r}}}r_{n}^{k}(t,z)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|r!\cdot k!\sum_{u=1}^{r\land k}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}\frac{r_{n}^{k-u}(t,z)}{(k-u)!}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{1}{l!}{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}r_{n}(t,z)\Big)^{k_{l}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\sum_{u=1}^{r\land k}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}\Big(|t|^{m+1}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\Big)^{k-u}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\Big(|t|^{m-l+1}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\Big)^{k_{l}}

\displaystyle=c(m)\sum_{u=1}^{r\land k}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}|t|^{(m+1)(k-u)}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{k-u}|t|^{(m+1)u-r}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{u}

\displaystyle=c(m)|t|^{(m+1)k-r}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{k}

and for
a,k=0,\dots,m
and
v=0,\dots,3m
by the Leibniz rule,

\begin{split}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}}\big(r_{n}^{k}(t,z)\ t^{v-(m+1)k}\big)\Big|&\leq c(m)\sum_{b=0}^{a}\Big(|t|^{(m+1)k-b}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{k}\Big)\Big(|t|^{v-(m+1)k-a+b}\Big)\\
&\leq c(m)|t|^{v-a}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{k}.\\
\end{split}

(3.23)

In
3.16
the summand with
u=0
of the first sum and the 1 cancel each other out, so we analyse the remaining first sum using
3.22

\displaystyle\sum_{u=1}^{m-1}\frac{v_{n}^{u}(t,z)}{u!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{u!}\Big(\sum_{l=1}^{m-2}\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}(it)^{l+2}z^{l}+r_{n}(t,z)z^{m-1}\Big)^{u}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{m-1}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-1)}\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}(it)^{l+2}z^{l}\Big)^{k_{l}}\;\frac{1}{k_{m-1}!}\Big(r_{n}(t,z)z^{m-1}\Big)^{k_{m-1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{m-1}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-1)}\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}\;\frac{1}{k_{m-1}!}\Big(r_{n}(t,z)\Big)^{k_{m-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot(it)^{r(k_{\cdot})+2u-(m+1)k_{m-1}}\,z^{r(k_{\cdot})}.

Let
r(k_{\cdot})\leq m-2
, so particularly
u\leq r\leq m-2
. Since
k_{r}
is the highest
k_{l}
that can be greater 0, also
k_{r+1}=k_{r+2}=\dots=k_{m-1}=0
. After shortening the term, we see that instead of summing over
u
, we can sum over
r
as well without changing any summand. This yields

\displaystyle\sum_{u=1}^{m-1}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-1)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{r(k_{\cdot})\leq m-2\}}\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}\;\frac{1}{k_{m-1}!}\Big(r_{n}(t,z)\Big)^{k_{m-1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot(it)^{r(k_{\cdot})+2u-(m+1)k_{m-1}}\,z^{r(k_{\cdot})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{m-2}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-2)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{r(k_{\cdot})\leq m-2\}}\prod_{l=1}^{r(k_{\cdot})}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}(it)^{r(k_{\cdot})+2u}\,z^{r(k_{\cdot})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{m-2}\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,u)}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}(it)^{r+2u}\,z^{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}(it)^{r+2u(k_{\cdot})}\,z^{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}\,z^{r}.

As a result, in
3.16
everything is eliminated except for
c(m)
many summands from the first sum, having the form

c(m)\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}^{k_{l}}\;r_{n}(t,z)^{k_{m-1}}(it)^{r(k_{\cdot})+2u-(m+1)k_{m-1}}\,z^{r(k_{\cdot})}

(3.24)

for some
k_{1},\dots,k_{m-1}\geq 0,\sum_{l=1}^{m-1}lk_{l}=r(k_{\cdot})\geq m-1,u=\sum_{l=1}^{m-1}k_{l},1\leq u\leq m-1
. The sum over these
k_{i}
will be denoted by
\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-1)*}
.
The cumulants are upper bounded by constants times the absolute moments, so by
Lemma

3.3
,

\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}=\frac{\sum\widetilde{\kappa}_{l+2,j}}{V_{n}^{l+2}}\leq c(l)\frac{\sum|X_{j}|^{l+2}}{V_{n}^{l+2}}=c(l)\widetilde{L}_{l+2,n}\leq c(l)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{l/(m-1)}

for
l=1,\dots,m-1
. For the second part of
3.24
, we use
3.23
(with
k=k_{m-1}
and
{v=r+2u}
) and get by
3.13

\begin{split}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}}R_{n,1}(t,z)\Big|&\leq c(m)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-1)*}\prod_{l=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{k_{l}\cdot l/(m-1)}|t|^{r(k_{\cdot})+2u-a}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{k_{m-1}}\,z^{r(k_{\cdot})}\\
&\leq c(m)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-1)*}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{r(k_{\cdot})/(m-1)}|t|^{r(k_{\cdot})+2u-a}\\
&\leq c(m)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-1)*}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\Big(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{1/(m-1)}|t|\Big)^{r(k_{\cdot})-(m-1)}|t|^{m-1+2u-a}\\
&\leq c(m)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},\cdot,u,m-1)*}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}|t|^{m-1+2u-a}\\
&\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\Big(|t|^{m+1-a}+|t|^{3m-3-a}\Big)\end{split}

(3.25)

for
a=0,\dots,m
.
Combining
3.15
,
3.21
and
3.25
gives

\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}}R_{n}(t,z)\Big|\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\Big(|t|^{m+1-a}+|t|^{3m-1-a}\Big)e^{t^{2}/3}

for
a=0,\dots,m
and by the Leibniz rule

\displaystyle\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\Big(e^{-t^{2}/2}R_{n}(t,z)\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\sum_{l=0}^{k}(1+|t|^{l})e^{-t^{2}/2}\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\Big(|t|^{m+1-(k-l)}+|t|^{3m-1-(k-l)}\Big)e^{t^{2}/3}

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}e^{-t^{2}/6}\Big(|t|^{m+1-k}+|t|^{3m-1+k}\Big)

for
k=0,\dots,m
. By
3.5
, we get

\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(tz)^{1/z^{2}}-e^{-t^{2}/2}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)z^{r}\Big)=e^{-t^{2}/2}R_{n}(t,z)

and by differentiating
k
times and setting
z=1
, this implies

\displaystyle\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\Big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)-e^{-t^{2}/2}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)\Big)\Big)\Big|\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}e^{-t^{2}/6}\Big(|t|^{m+1-k}+|t|^{3m-1+k}\Big)

for
k=0,\dots,m
.
∎

In the next step, we prove the non-uniform bound for our Edgeworth expansion in the conditional setting.
Recall the definition
\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)=\Phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{P}_{r,n}(x)
, where

\widetilde{P}_{r,n}(x)=-\phi(x)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}H_{r+2u(k_{\cdot})-1}(x)\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}

from
2.9
and
2.8
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric,
V_{n}^{2}>0
and
m\geq 2
is an integer. Then

\begin{split}&\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)|\leq c(m)\Big(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+I_{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{m}I_{k,2}+e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}\Big),\end{split}

(3.26)

where
I_{2}=\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{-1}\big|\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t
and for
k=0,\dots,m
,

I_{k,2}=\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{k-m-1}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t

.

First, we use Lemma 8 with
F=\widetilde{F}_{n}
and
G=\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}
,
T=\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}
and Lemma 7 with
G=\widetilde{F}_{n}-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}
from
[
38
, Chapter VI]
, each applied with conditional Fourier transforms and finite
m
-th conditional moment. Next,
{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}}\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}=\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}
, thus by
2.14
and
2.19
,

\big(1+|x|\big)^{m}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}}\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\Big|=\big(1+|x|\big)^{m}\big|\phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\big|=\big(1+|x|\big)^{m}\Big|\phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{p}_{r,n}(x)\Big|\leq c(m)

uniformly for all
x
and
n
and all conditions of the lemmas are satisfied.
Let

\delta_{m}(t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{itx}\,\mathrm{d}\big(x^{m}(\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x))\big)\,.

Now the lemmas yield

\displaystyle\big|\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\big|

\displaystyle\leq c(m)(1+|x|)^{-m}\Big(\int_{-\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}^{\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}\Big|\frac{\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)}{t}\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{-\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}^{\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}\Big|\frac{\delta_{m}(t)}{t}\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{c(m)}{\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq c(m)(1+|x|)^{-m}\Big(\int_{-\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}^{\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{-1}\big|\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=0}^{m}\int_{-\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}^{\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{k-m-1}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t+c(m)\,\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\Big)

\displaystyle=c(m)(1+|x|)^{-m}\Big(\underbrace{\int_{-\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}^{\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{-1}\big|\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=I}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=0}^{m}\underbrace{\int_{|t|<B_{n}}|t|^{k-m-1}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=I_{k,1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=0}^{m}\underbrace{\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{k-m-1}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=I_{k,2}}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{k=0}^{m}\underbrace{\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{k-m-1}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=I_{k,3}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+c(m)\,\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\Big)

for
x\in\mathbb{R}
with
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}
from
2.7
.

Since in our conditional setting all moments exist, we can deduce from
Proposition

3.2

\displaystyle I_{k,1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|t|<B_{n}}|t|^{k-m-1}c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}e^{-t^{2}/6}\big(|t|^{m+1-k}+|t|^{3m-1+k}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}\int_{|t|<B_{n}}e^{-t^{2}/6}\big(1+|t|^{2m-2+2k}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}

for
k=0,\dots,m
.

Now we examine
I_{k,3}
.
By
2.17
and since
|t|\geq B_{n}\geq 1
,

\begin{split}I_{k,3}&=\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{k-m-1}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq c(m)\int_{|t|\geq B_{n}}|t|^{m-5+2k}e^{-t^{2}/2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq c(m)e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}\end{split}

(3.28)

for
k=0,\dots,m
, where we use
Lemma

A.4
(with
\beta=1
,
\nu_{n}=B_{n}
) in the last step.

In the same manner we proceed for
I
and get

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=\int_{-\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}^{\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{-1}\big|\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|t|<B_{n}}c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}e^{-t^{2}/6}\big(|t|^{m}+|t|^{3m-2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{-1}\Big|\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=I_{2}}\ +\ \int_{|t|\geq B_{n}}\Big|e^{-t^{2}/2}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)\Big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+I_{2}+c(m)e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}.

Inserting everything in
3.1
, we arrive at

\big|\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\big|\leq c(m)(1+|x|)^{-m}\Big(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+I_{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{m}I_{k,2}+e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}\Big)

for
x\in\mathbb{R}
, which can be rewritten as
3.26
.
∎

3.2
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Before proving the main theorem, we formulate some auxiliary propositions in which we bound expectation values of summands from
3.26
separately.

The proof of the following proposition involves thorough investigation and precise bounding of integral terms. The crucial point here is that
B_{n}
is random and its expectation is smaller in order than
n^{-1/2}
for our self-normalized sum
T_{n}
. In contrast, for the classical CLT-statistic
Z_{n}
, the integration interval starts at a deterministic point of order
n^{-1/2}
. Our larger integration interval complicates the bounding of the integral enormously and finally leads to the logarithmic factor in the remainder of
Theorem

1.1
. The randomness in our integral, combined with the expectation results in a proof that differs fundamentally from usual proofs for Edgeworth expansions.

Proposition 3.5
.

Assume that the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular and
{\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{s}<\infty}
for some
{s}\geq 2
. Let
\nu_{n},\tau_{n}\to\infty
in polynomial order, bounded by
\nu_{n},\tau_{n}\leq n^{c}
for some
{c=c(s)}
. Then for
m=\lfloor{s}\rfloor
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}\int_{B_{n}\leq|u|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos\big(uV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg]

\displaystyle\quad=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+\max\{2r,1\})/2}\big)

holds for all
r=0,\dots,m
.

The following lemma treats the expectation of mixed moments (with an additional term) and is required in the proof of
Proposition

3.5
. Its proof can be found in
Section

A.2
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Assume that
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{{s}}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
. Then for all
l=1,\dots,\lfloor s\rfloor
,
{1\leq a_{i}\leq{s}}
for
i=1,\dots,l
, and
1\leq\eta\leq c(s)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}|X_{1}|^{a_{1}}\cdots|X_{l}|^{a_{l}}\Big]=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\Big(n^{-({s}-\max\{2,a_{1},\dots,a_{l}\})/2}\,(\log n)^{{s}/2}\,\eta^{{s}/2}\Big).

Remark 3.7

(Moment assumptions)
.

Assuming finite moments (
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{{s}}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
) provides useful truncation inequalities. We truncate the maximum
M_{n}
at
a_{n}\to\infty
and get by Markov’s inequality

\mathbb{P}\big(M_{n}\geq a_{n}\big)\leq n\,\mathbb{P}\big(|X_{1}|\geq a_{n}\big)\leq n\,a_{n}^{-{s}}\int_{|x|\geq a_{n}}|x|^{{s}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X_{1}}(x)=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n\,a_{n}^{-{s}}\big).

(3.29)

Note
that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{|X_{1}|\geq n^{1/4}\}}|X_{1}|^{s}\big]\longrightarrow 0

for
n\to\infty
. Let
(\delta_{n})
be a sequence that satisfies
n^{-1/4}\leq\delta_{n}<1
and
\delta_{n}\to 0
sufficiently slowly that

\displaystyle\delta_{n}^{-s}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{|X_{1}|\geq n^{1/4}\}}|X_{1}|^{s}\Big]\longrightarrow 0.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{|X_{1}|\geq\delta_{n}\sqrt{n}\,\}}\delta_{n}^{-s}|X_{1}|^{s}\Big]\leq\delta_{n}^{-s}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{|X_{1}|\geq n^{-1/4}\sqrt{n}\,\}}|X_{1}|^{s}\Big]\longrightarrow 0

(3.30)

and thus

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\big(M_{n}\geq\delta_{n}\sqrt{n}\,\big)&\leq n\,(\delta_{n}\sqrt{n}\,)^{-{s}}\int_{|x|\geq\delta_{n}\sqrt{n}}|x|^{{s}}\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X_{1}}(x)\\
&=n^{-(s-2)/2}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{|X_{1}|\geq\delta_{n}\sqrt{n}\,\}}\delta_{n}^{-s}|X_{1}|^{s}\Big]=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(s-2)/2}\big).\end{split}

(3.31)

Additionally by
[
38
, Theorem 28, Chapter IX]
,

\mathbb{P}\big(|V_{n}^{2}-n\mu_{2}|\geq\varepsilon n\big)=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)

for all
\varepsilon>0
and particularly (by choosing
\varepsilon=\mu_{2}/2=1/2
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(V_{n}^{2}\leq n/2\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\big(|V_{n}^{2}-n|\geq n/2\big)=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

(3.32)

For the sake of legibility, we suppress the notation of the indicator
\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}
which is denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{*}[\,\cdot\,]:=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}\,\cdot\,]
throughout this proof. Every time these bounds are used, we will mention it explicitly. In the following set of inequalities, we use the indicator. By
V_{n}\geq M_{n}>\zeta
and Fubini’s theorem, we get

\begin{split}\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\int_{B_{n}\leq|u|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos\big(uV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg]$\mbox{}\hfil\quad\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\int_{M_{n}^{-1}\leq|u|<\nu_{n}V_{n}^{-1}}V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos(u|X_{j}|)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg]\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\int_{0\leq|u|<\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{M_{n}^{-1}\leq|u|\}}V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos(u|X_{j}|)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg]\\
&=\int_{0\leq|u|<\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{*}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{M_{n}^{-1}\leq|u|\}}V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos(u|X_{j}|)\Big|\Big]\,\mathrm{d}u.\end{split}

(3.33)

Subsequently, we deduce rather tight
u
-dependent bounds on the
\mathbb{E}_{*}
expression within the integral. To this end, we derive precise bounds on the derivatives of the product inside.

The higher derivative of a product can be evaluated by the generalized Leibniz formula which states in our case

{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos(u|X_{j}|)=\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{n}=r}{r\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\prod\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i_{j}}}{\mathrm{d}u^{i_{j}}}}\cos(u|X_{j}|)\Big)\,.

Here the sum extends over all
n
-tuples
(i_{1},...,i_{n})
of non-negative integers with
\sum_{j=1}^{n}i_{j}=r
and

{r\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}={\frac{r!}{i_{1}!\,i_{2}!\cdots i_{n}!}}.

Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are i.i.d.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[{r\choose i_{\pi(1)},\ldots,i_{\pi(n)}}\prod\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i_{\pi(j)}}}{\mathrm{d}u^{i_{\pi(j)}}}}\cos(u|X_{j}|)\Big)\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[{r\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\prod\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i_{j}}}{\mathrm{d}u^{i_{j}}}}\cos(u|X_{j}|)\Big)\Big]

for all (non-random) permutations
\pi
from
\{1,\dots,n\}
to itself.

For
l=1,\dots,r
, let
k_{l}
be the number of appearances of
l
in the tuple
(i_{1},...,i_{n})
(so
\sum_{l=1}^{r}lk_{l}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}i_{j}=r
). Then for each tuple
(k_{1},\dots,k_{r})
, there exist
{n\choose k_{1},\ldots,k_{r},(n-\sum_{l=1}^{r}k_{l})}
tuples
(i_{1},...,i_{n})
such that
k_{l}
is the number of appearances of
l
in the tuple
(i_{1},...,i_{n})
. So instead of summing over all
n
-tuples
(i_{1},...,i_{n})
with
\sum_{j=1}^{n}i_{j}=r
, we can sum over all
r
-tuples
(k_{1},...,k_{r})
of non-negative integers with
\sum_{l=1}^{r}lk_{l}=r
and multiply each summand by
{n\choose k_{1},\ldots,k_{r},(n-\sum_{l=1}^{r}k_{l})}
. We perform the just described summation by using the sum
\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}
and
u(k_{\cdot})
which are explained in
Section

2
. Additionally, we convert

{r\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}={\frac{r!}{i_{1}!\,i_{2}!\cdots i_{n}!}}=\frac{r!}{1!^{k_{1}}\cdots r!^{k_{r}}}.

Now for any
(k_{1},\dots,k_{r})
, we identify that element of the corresponding set

\{(i_{\pi(1)},...,i_{\pi(n)}):\pi\text{ permutations from }\{1,\dots,n\}\text{ to itself}\}

for which

\displaystyle i_{1}

\displaystyle=\dots=i_{k_{1}}=1,

\displaystyle i_{k_{1}+1}

\displaystyle=\dots=i_{k_{1}+k_{2}}=2,

\displaystyle i_{\sum_{l=1}^{r-1}k_{l}+1}

\displaystyle=\dots=i_{\sum_{l=1}^{r}k_{l}}=r,

\displaystyle i_{\sum_{l=1}^{r}k_{l}+1}

\displaystyle=\dots=i_{n}=0.

When summing over
(k_{1},\dots,k_{r})
, this will be the representative, evaluated within the expectation.
As
V_{n}^{2}
and
M_{n}
are invariant under permutation, we convert the above expression
3.33
with
A_{n}:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{M_{n}^{-1}\leq|u|\}}V_{n}^{-(r-1)}
into

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\{M_{n}^{-1}\leq|u|\}}V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos(u|X_{j}|)\Big|\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[A_{n}\cdot\Big|\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{n}=r}{r\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\prod\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i_{j}}}{\mathrm{d}u^{i_{j}}}}\cos(u|X_{n}|)\Big)\Big|\bigg]

(3.34)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[A_{n}\cdot\Big|\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}{n\choose k_{1},\ldots,k_{r},(n-u(k_{\cdot}))}\frac{r!}{1!^{k_{1}}\cdots r!^{k_{r}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{r}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{l}}\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}u^{l}}}\cos\big(u|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{l-1}k_{b}+a}|\big)\Big)\Big)\prod_{l=u(k_{\cdot})+1}^{n}\cos(u|X_{l}|)\Big|\bigg].

In the next step we apply the triangle inequality and evaluate the derivates of these cosine-structures which result in multiplying by the respective
|X_{j}|
and switching to the sine for uneven derivatives. Overall we get

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[A_{n}\cdot\Big|\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}{n\choose k_{1},\ldots,k_{r},(n-u(k_{\cdot}))}\frac{r!}{1!^{k_{1}}\cdots r!^{k_{r}}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{r}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{l}}{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}u^{l}}}\cos\big(u|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{l-1}k_{b}+a}|\big)\Big)\prod_{l=u(k_{\cdot})+1}^{n}\cos(u|X_{l}|)\Big|\bigg]\\
&\quad\leq\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[A_{n}\,c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}n^{u(k_{\cdot})}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{l}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}u^{l}}}\cos\big(u|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{l-1}k_{b}+a}|\big)\Big|\Big)\prod_{l=u(k_{\cdot})+1}^{n}\big|\cos(u|X_{l}|)\big|\bigg]\end{split}

(3.35)

\begin{split}&\quad=c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}n^{u(k_{\cdot})}\,\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[A_{n}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l-1}\Big|\sin\big(u\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|\big)\Big|\Big)\\
&\quad\qquad\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big|\cos\big(u\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|\big)\Big|\Big)\prod_{l=u(k_{\cdot})+1}^{n}\big|\cos(u|X_{l}|)\big|\bigg].\end{split}

Since
|\sin|
and
|\cos|
are symmetric around 0, we can only work with positive
u
and multiply by 2 (which is included in
c(r)
). Thus in
3.33
,
3.2
and
3.35
, we showed

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\int_{B_{n}\leq|u|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos\big(uV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg]\leq c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}n^{u(k_{\cdot})}\int_{0\leq u<\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}}\\
&\qquad\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\{M_{n}^{-1}\leq u\}}V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l-1}\Big|\sin\big(u\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|\big)\Big|\Big)\\
&\qquad\quad\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big|\cos\big(u\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|\big)\Big|\Big)\prod_{l=u(k_{\cdot})+1}^{n}\big|\cos(u|X_{l}|)\big|\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}u\\
&=:W_{1}+W_{2},\end{split}

(3.36)

where
W_{1}
represents the integral over
u\in\big[0,\sqrt{n^{-1}\log(n)\,\eta}\big)
and
W_{2}
represents the integral over
u\in\big[\sqrt{n^{-1}\log(n)\,\eta},\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}\big)
with

\eta:=6\big(2r+{s}+4c\big).

Note
1\leq\eta\leq c(s)
. Our choice of the split point
\sqrt{n^{-1}\log(n)\,\eta}
is motivated by the bounds in
3.43
and
3.44
.

First, we focus on a bound on
W_{1}
. Here, our basic idea is to simplify the complex expectation in
W_{1}
to allow using
Lemma

3.6
. The bounds we use have to be particularly tight for summands where
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
are large (i.e.
k_{3}=\dots=k_{r}=0
) as these summands result in the largest powers of
n
. We bound
|\cos(x)|\leq 1
and
\sin(x)\leq x
for
x\geq 0
. Thus,

\displaystyle W_{1}

\displaystyle\leq c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}n^{u(k_{\cdot})}\int_{0\leq u<\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\{M_{n}^{-1}\leq u\}}V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l-1}\Big|\big(u\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|\big)\Big|\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big)\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}u.

We replace the integral by the supremum of the integrand multiplied with the interval length, which bounds the expression above by

\displaystyle c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}n^{u(k_{\cdot})}n^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}}\big(\log(n)\,\eta\big)^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}}\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}\,\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}^{-1}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}\big\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big)\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}\hskip-3.44444ptn^{\frac{1}{2}(-1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}+2\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l})}\big(\log(n)\,\eta\big)^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1})}\hskip 0.86108pt\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big)\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=:W_{3}.

Hence,
W_{1}\leq W_{3}
. For
r=0
,
W_{3}
reduces significantly. Using
3.29
and
Lemma

3.6
(
l=1
,
a_{1}=2
) yields

\displaystyle W_{3}

\displaystyle=c(0)\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}\;\mathbb{E}_{*}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}V_{n}\Big]

(3.37)

\displaystyle\leq c(0)\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{2}}\;\mathbb{E}_{*}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{V_{n}^{2}\leq\frac{n}{2}\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+c(0)\sqrt{\tfrac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{n}}\;\mathbb{E}_{*}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{V_{n}^{2}>\frac{n}{2}\}}V_{n}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq c(0)\sqrt{\log(n)\,\eta}\;\mathbb{E}_{*}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}\Big]+c(0)\sqrt{\log(n)\,\eta}\;\mathbb{E}_{*}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}X_{1}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,\log(n)^{({s}+1)/2}\big).

For
r\geq 1
, we can simplify
W_{3}
by using
|X_{j}|\leq V_{n}
,

\displaystyle c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}\hskip-3.87495ptn^{\frac{1}{2}(-1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}+2\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l})}\big(\log(n)\,\eta\big)^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1})}\,\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big)\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2l}\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}\hskip-3.87495ptn^{\frac{1}{2}(-1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}+2\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l})}\big(\log(n)\,\eta\big)^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1})}\,\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot V_{n}^{-(r-1)}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2}V_{n}^{2l-2}\Big)\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2}V_{n}^{2l-2}\Big)\bigg].

The power of
V_{n}
in the respective summands is

\displaystyle-(r-1)+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}(2l-2)+\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l}(2l-2)

\displaystyle={1-\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}-2\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l}}

such that the above expression equals

\displaystyle c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}n^{\frac{1}{2}(-1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}+2\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l})}\big(\log(n)\,\eta\big)^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1})}\,\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot V_{n}^{1-\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}-2\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l}}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2}\Big)\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2}\Big)\bigg].

As
r\geq 1
, we know that
k_{l}>0
for some
l=1,\dots,r
such that
V_{n}
has a non-positive power. Thus, using
V_{n}\geq M_{n}
, we can bound the above expression by

\displaystyle c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}n^{\frac{1}{2}(-1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}+2\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l})}\big(\log(n)\,\eta\big)^{\frac{1}{2}(1+\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1})}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Big(\sqrt{\tfrac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\,\Big)^{1-\sum_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}k_{2l-1}-2\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}k_{2l}}\,\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2}\Big)\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2}\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}\big(\log(n)\,\eta\big)^{u(k_{\cdot})}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\big\{M_{n}\geq\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(n)\,\eta}}\big\}}\prod_{l=1}^{\lceil r/2\rceil}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l-1}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{(2l-1)-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2}\Big)\prod_{l=1}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}\Big(\prod_{a=1}^{k_{2l}}\big|X_{\sum_{b=1}^{2l-1}k_{b}+a}\big|^{2}\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+2r)/2}\big)

where we used
Lemma

3.6
(
l=u(k_{\cdot}),a_{1}=\dots=a_{u(k_{\cdot})}=2
) in the last step. Thus, for
r\geq 1
,

W_{1}\leq W_{3}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+2r)/2}\big).

(3.38)

After having dealt with
W_{1}
, we direct our attention to bounding the other part of
3.36
,
W_{2}
. Due to the area of integration in
W_{2}
,
u
now is larger and thus,
\cos(u|X_{\cdot}|)
is smaller than in the integral in
W_{1}
. Therefore, our basic idea is to show that the expectation of the cosines are sufficiently small and to use their
(n-u(k_{\cdot}))
times product
\big|\cos(u|X_{u(k_{\cdot})+1}|)\big|\cdots\big|\cos(u|X_{n}|)\big|
to bound
W_{2}
. In the process, we use
\sin(x)\leq 1
and
\cos(x)\leq 1
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
|X_{j}|/V_{n}\leq 1
for all
j=1,\dots,n
. This leads to

\displaystyle W_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c(r)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}n^{u(k_{\cdot})}\int_{\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}\leq u<\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[V_{n}\prod_{l=u(k_{\cdot})+1}^{n}\big|\cos(u|X_{l}|)\big|\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}u

(3.39)

\displaystyle\leq c(r)n^{r}\int_{\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}\leq u<\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[V_{n}\prod_{l=r+1}^{n}\big|\cos(u|X_{l}|)\big|\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}u.

Using the indicator
\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}
implies
V_{n}^{2}\leq nM_{n}^{2}\leq n\tau_{n}^{2}
such that we can eliminate the
V_{n}
and since the
X_{j}
are i.i.d., by defining
\mathcal{U}_{n}:=\big[\sqrt{n^{-1}\log(n)\,\eta},\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}\big]
, we get

\displaystyle c(r)n^{r}\int_{\sqrt{\frac{\log(n)\,\eta}{n}}\leq u<\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}}\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[V_{n}\prod_{l=r+1}^{n}\big|\cos(u|X_{l}|)\big|\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq c(r)n^{(2r+1)/2}\tau_{n}\,|\mathcal{U}_{n}|\,\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\prod_{l=r+1}^{n}\big|\cos(u|X_{l}|)\big|\bigg]

(3.40)

\displaystyle\leq c(r)n^{(2r+1)/2}\tau_{n}\,\nu_{n}\zeta^{-1}\,\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\prod_{l=r+1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\cos(u|X_{l}|)\big|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq c(r)n^{(2r+1)/2}\tau_{n}\,\nu_{n}\,\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\cos(u|X_{1}|)\big|\Big]^{n-r}.

To achieve the aspired rate of convergence for
W_{1}
, we will show that

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\cos(u|X_{1}|)\big|\Big]\leq 1-\frac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}

(3.41)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
