sufficiently large.
We conduct the proof by contradiction. Suppose
3.41
does not hold, meaning for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists some
n\geq N
for which there exists at least one
u_{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\cos\big(u_{n}|X_{1}|\big)\big|\Big]>1-\frac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}.

(3.42)

Let
(n_{k})
be a subsequence of these
n
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\cos\big(u_{n_{k}}|X_{1}|\big)\big|\Big]>1-\frac{\log(n_{k})\,\eta/6}{n_{k}}.

Hence, this sequence
(u_{n_{k}})
either has a convergent subsequence
(u_{n_{k_{l}}})
with
u_{n_{k_{l}}}\to u^{*}
for some
u^{*}\in[0,\infty)
or
u_{n_{k_{l}}}\to\infty
. Note
u_{n_{k_{l}}}>0
. For the ease of notation, we still denote the subsubsequence by
(u_{n})
. In consequence, we assume that for all members of this sequence,
3.42
holds. We subsequently distinguish the cases
u^{*}=0,u^{*}\in(0,\infty)
and
u_{n}\to\infty
.

Case 1 (
u^{*}=0
):

Due to
\cos(x)\leq 1-x^{2}/3
for
x\in(0,\tfrac{\pi}{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\cos\big(u_{n}|X_{1}|\big)\big|\Big]\leq 1-\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<u_{n}|X_{1}|<\pi/2\}}\frac{u_{n}^{2}|X_{1}|^{2}}{3}\bigg]=1-\frac{u_{n}^{2}\,\mu_{2,n}^{\prime}}{3},

where

\mu_{2,n}^{\prime}:=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<u_{n}|X_{1}|<\pi/2\}}|X_{1}|^{2}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{0<|X_{1}|<\frac{\pi}{2u_{n}}\}}|X_{1}|^{2}\Big]\longrightarrow 1

by dominated convergence. Therefore,
\mu_{2,n}^{\prime}\geq 1/2
for
n
sufficiently large. Since
u_{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
,
u_{n}\geq\sqrt{n^{-1}\log(n)\,\eta}
such that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\cos\big(u_{n}|X_{1}|\big)\Big]\leq 1-\frac{u_{n}^{2}\,\mu_{2,n}^{\prime}}{3}\leq 1-\frac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}

for
n
sufficiently large.

Case 2 (
u^{*}\in(0,\infty)
):

Due to non-singularity, the distribution of
|X_{1}|
cannot have mass in
\{j\,\pi/u^{*}\mid j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
only. By
Lemma

A.5
, there exists
\beta
with
0<\beta<1
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\cos(u^{*}|X_{1}|)\big|\Big]=\beta.

So due to continuity of the cosine and dominated convergence, for all
\beta^{\prime}
with
\beta<\beta^{\prime}<1
, there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\cos(u_{n}|X_{1}|)\big|\Big]\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq 1-\frac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}

holds for all
n\geq N
.

Case 3 (
u_{n}\to\infty
):

By non-singularity, we know that
p_{ac}>0
(see
Remark

3.1
), so we first focus on the absolute continuous part
F_{ac}
of the distribution of
X_{1}
.

By the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma,

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp(iu_{n}x)\,\mathrm{d}F_{ac}(x)\Big|\longrightarrow 0

for
u_{n}\to\infty
. Thus for the real part, we know

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\cos(u_{n}x)\,\mathrm{d}F_{ac}(x)\longrightarrow 0

for
u_{n}\to\infty
. Since the cosine is symmetric, this yields

\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(\cos(2u_{n}|x|)+1\big)\,\mathrm{d}F_{ac}(x)\longrightarrow\tfrac{1}{2}

for
u_{n}\to\infty
. By Jensen’s inequality and the trigonometric equation
\cos(2\alpha)=2\cos(\alpha)^{2}-1

[
47
, 6.5.10]
,

\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\cos(u_{n}|x|)|\,\mathrm{d}F_{ac}(x)\Big)^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\cos(u_{n}|x|)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}F_{ac}(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\cos(2u_{n}|x|)+1\,\mathrm{d}F_{ac}(x).

The right-hand side converges to
1/2
for
u_{n}\to\infty
. Due to the Lebesgue decomposition of the distribution function
3.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[|\cos(u_{n}|X_{1}|)|\big]

\displaystyle=p_{ac}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\cos(u_{n}|x|)|\,\mathrm{d}F_{ac}(x)+p_{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\cos(u_{n}|x|)|\,\mathrm{d}F_{d}(x)+p_{sc}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\cos(u_{n}|x|)|\,\mathrm{d}F_{sc}(x)

\displaystyle\leq p_{ac}\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\cos(2u_{n}|x|)+1\,\mathrm{d}F_{ac}(x)}+p_{d}+p_{sc}\longrightarrow\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}p_{ac}+p_{d}+p_{sc}<1

for
u_{n}\to\infty
. Hence, for any
\beta^{\prime}
with
p_{ac}/\sqrt{2}+p_{d}+p_{sc}<\beta^{\prime}<1
, there consequently exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbb{E}\big[|\cos(u_{n}|X_{1}|)|\big]\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq 1-\frac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}

holds for all
n\geq N
.

As demonstrated,
3.42
leads to a contradiction in all three cases. Thus, we have shown
3.41
by contradiction.

Since
\log(1-x)\leq-x
for all
x<1
, for
n\geq 2m
and
n>\log(n)\,\eta/6
, we can write

\begin{split}\Big(1-\tfrac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}\Big)^{n-r}&\leq\Big(1-\tfrac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}\Big)^{n/2}=\exp\Big(\tfrac{n}{2}\,\log\big(1-\tfrac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}\big)\Big)\\
&\leq\exp\Big(\tfrac{n}{2}\big(-\tfrac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}\big)\Big)=n^{-\eta/12}.\end{split}

(3.43)

Recall
\nu_{n},\tau_{n}\leq n^{c}
and
\eta=6(2r+{s}+4c)
. Thus, by
3.39
,
3.2
,
3.41
and
3.43

\displaystyle W_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c(r)n^{(2r+1)/2}\tau_{n}\,\nu_{n}\,\Big(1-\frac{\log(n)\,\eta/6}{n}\Big)^{n-r}

(3.44)

\displaystyle\leq c(r)n^{(2r+1)/2}\tau_{n}\,\nu_{n}\,n^{-(2r+{s}+4c)/2}=\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-({s}-1)/2}\big).

We finish the proof by merging
3.36
,
3.37
,
3.38
and
3.44
to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{*}\bigg[\int_{B_{n}\leq|u|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{r}}{\mathrm{d}u^{r}}}\prod\cos\big(uV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}u\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq W_{1}+W_{2}\leq W_{3}+W_{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+\max\{2r,1\})/2}\big).

∎

In the next lemma, we bound the expectation of
\widetilde{L}_{k,n}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Assume that
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{{s}}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
. Then for all
k>{s}
,

\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{L}_{k,n}]=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

The expectation of the remaining summand, an exponential of
B_{n}
, is bounded in the lemma below.

Lemma 3.9
.

Assume that
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{{s}}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
. Then for all
\eta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\big(-\eta\,B_{n}^{2}\big)\right]=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{{s}/2}(\eta\wedge 1)^{-s/2}\big).

The rather straightforward proofs of
Lemmas

3.8
and
3.9
are deferred to
Section

A.2
. The following remark is the last required component for the proof of the main theorem.

Remark 3.10
.

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

T_{n}^{2}=\frac{S_{n}^{2}}{V_{n}^{2}}=\frac{(\sum X_{j})^{2}}{\sum X_{j}^{2}}\leq\frac{n\sum X_{j}^{2}}{\sum X_{j}^{2}}=n,

that is,
|T_{n}|\leq\sqrt{n}
.

As
|T_{n}|\leq\sqrt{n}
,
F_{n}(x)=0
for
x<-\sqrt{n}
and
F_{n}(x)=1
for
x>\sqrt{n}
. Due to the form
1.1
and the factor
\phi
in all expansion terms
{Q}_{r}
,

\sup_{x>\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\Big(\big|\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(-x)\big|+\big|1-\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)\big|\Big)=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

Therefore, the left-hand side of
1.3
is of order
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)
for the supremum restricted to
|x|>\sqrt{n}
. That is why we only have to consider the supremum over
|x|\leq\sqrt{n}
throughout this proof.

In order to apply
Proposition

3.4
, the left-hand side of
1.3
has to be converted into a form related to the left-hand side of
3.26
. By
\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)]=F_{n}(x)
, Jensen’s inequality and
Proposition

2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|F_{n}(x)-\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|x|\leq\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\right]\right|+\sup_{|x|\leq\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\right]-\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{|x|\leq\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\right|\right]+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

Due to
|x|\leq\sqrt{n}
, the maximum order the factor
(1+|x|)^{m}
can take is
n^{m/2}
. Next,
\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}
from
2.8
has the property

\sup_{n}\|\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}\|_{\sup}\leq c(m)

due to
2.18
.
Thus, the absolute difference
\|\widetilde{F}_{n}-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}\|_{\sup}
is bounded by a constant
c(m)
.
By
3.29
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(M_{n}\geq n)=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-1)}\big).

Let
\zeta>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(|X_{1}|\leq\zeta)<1
. Then,

\mathbb{P}(M_{n}\leq\zeta)=\mathbb{P}(|X_{1}|\leq\zeta)^{n}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m+{s}-2)/2}\big)

in particular. Therefore, decomposing into
\{M_{n}\leq\zeta\},\{\zeta<M_{n}<n\}
and
\{M_{n}\geq n\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{|x|\leq\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<n\}}\sup_{|x|\leq\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\right|\right]+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

Recall
B_{n}=V_{n}/M_{n}
. Applying
Proposition

3.4
(
M_{n}>\zeta
guarantees
V_{n}^{2}>0
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<n\}}\sup_{|x|\leq\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{F}_{n}(x)-\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}(x)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<n\}}\Big(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+I_{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{m}I_{k,2}+e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}\Big)\right],

where
I_{2}=\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{-1}\big|\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t
and

I_{k,2}=\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}}|t|^{k-m-1}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t.

Regarding the first summand,

\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}]=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)

by
Lemma

3.8
with
m+1>{s}
.
Concerning
I_{2}
and
I_{k,2}
, we first bound the upper endpoint of the integration interval
\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}
by

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}^{-1}=V_{n}^{m+1}\Big(\sum|X_{j}|^{m+1}\Big)^{-1}\leq\big(n\,M_{n}^{2}\big)^{(m+1)/2}\big(M_{n}^{m+1}\big)^{-1}=n^{(m+1)/2}=:\nu_{n}.

Note that
\nu_{n}
is deterministic.
Next, we can leave out negative powers of
|t|
since
|t|\geq B_{n}\geq 1
. Then we get from
Proposition

3.5
(with
\tau_{n}=n
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<n\}}I_{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<n\}}\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\Big|\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg]

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+1)/2}\big)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<n\}}I_{k,2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<n\}}\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg]

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+\max\{2k,1\})/2}\big)

for all
k=0,\dots,m
.

Lemma

3.9
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}\right]=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{{s}/2}\big).

Therefore
1.3
holds and
Theorem

1.1
is proved.
∎

4
Non-uniform bounds for Edgeworth expansions in local limit theorems for self-normalized sums

This section is devoted to proving
Theorem

1.2
and more general LLT-type results for self-normalized sums. The crucial obstacle as compared to the CLT-setting is that, conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, the self-normalized sum
T_{n}
does not have a density with respect to the Lebesgue measure. Instead it is continuous with respect to the counting measure. Therefore, the Fourier inversion formula does not apply any longer.

We introduce the following condition, which will be imposed in
Theorem

4.1
. It is particularly satisfied if
X_{1}
has a bounded density, see
Proposition

4.2
. For instance, it has been successfully applied in
[
34
]
to derive a saddle-point approximation for
T_{n}
.

(C1)

The joint characteristic function
\varphi_{(X_{1},X_{1}^{2})}
of
(X_{1},X_{1}^{2})
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\varphi_{(X_{1},X_{1}^{2})}(t)|^{c}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty

for some
c\geq 1
.

1
is a smoothness condition for the corresponding density. By
[
8
, Theorem 19.1]
, it is equivalent to
(\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j},\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{j}^{2})

having bounded densities for
n
sufficiently large.
In this case,
p_{d}=0
(see
3.1
) holds necessarily because any discrete part cannot not vanish when summing up i.i.d. random variables (see
[
34
, Proposition 6.1]
).

1
implies Cramér condition (see
[
30
, p. 78]
), but it does not imply non-singularity and neither does non-singularity imply
1
(see
[
8
, p. 192]
).

Recall
\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)=\phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}
from
1.5
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular,
1
is satisfied and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 3
. Then there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N
, the statistics
T_{n}
have densities
f_{n}
that satisfy

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-3)/2}\big).

(4.1)

Moreover, if
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m+2}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N},m\geq 2
, the order of convergence reduces to
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\,\log n\big)
.

Note that
4.1
implies that the densities
f_{n}
are uniformly bounded for
n\geq N
. For the normalized sum
Z_{n}
, a similar non-uniform bound can be found in
[
12
]
. Together with the following
Proposition

4.2
,
Theorem

4.1
implies
Theorem

1.2
. Its rather technical proof can be found in
Section

A.3
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Assume that
X_{1}
has a bounded density. Then
1
holds.

Structure of the proof of
Theorem

4.1

As already mentioned, the starting point is again conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, leaving us with discrete
T_{n}
. Perturbing
T_{n}
(see
Section

4.1
) allows to derive an LLT in the conditional setting (
Proposition

4.5
) by bounding the characteristic functions (
Lemma

4.4
) and using the Fourier inversion formula. Afterwards, we return to the unconditional setting by taking expectations (
Proposition

4.6
). The necessary deconvolution leads to
Proposition

4.7
, which applies under
1
and is used in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

Remark 4.3
.

The one-dimensional analogue of
1
, namely
|\varphi_{1}|^{c}
being integrable for some
c\geq 1
, is equivalent to
Z_{n}
having a bounded density
f_{Z_{N}}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
(or equivalently for all sufficiently large
n
) by
[
8
, Theorem 19.1]
. This is necessary for any LLT for
Z_{n}
to hold and therefore posed as a condition in all LLTs such as
[
8
, Theorem 19.2]
,
[
12
]
and
[
38
, Theorem 17, Chapter VII]
.

4.1
The conditional setting

Given
|X_{1}|,\dots,|X_{n}|
, there are (at most)
2^{n}
possible values for
T_{n}
as each
|X_{j}|
can be included in
S_{n}
with positive or negative sign. This is denoted by
\pm_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,2^{n}
. Due to the symmetry of
X_{1}
, the self-normalized sum
T_{n}
(conditioned on
|X_{1}|,\dots,|X_{n}|
) takes each of these values with probability
2^{-n}
. Thus in the conditional setting,
T_{n}
does not have a density but the probability mass function

\widetilde{f}_{n}(x):=f_{T_{n}\mid|X_{1}|,\dots,|X_{n}|}\big(x\mid|X_{1}|,\dots,|X_{n}|\big)=2^{-n}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{V_{n}^{-1}{\sum\pm_{i}|X_{j}|}=x\}}\,.

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

As
\widetilde{f}_{n}
is not a Lebesgue density, the Fourier inversion formula does not apply. Therefore, we perturb
T_{n}
with an independent absolutely continuous random variable
\varepsilon_{n}
and examine

T_{n}^{\prime}:=T_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}.

For the distribution of
\varepsilon_{n}
, the normal distribution seems to be a suitable choice. In contrast to other distributions, the tails of its density and characteristic function decay in the same magnitude. As we operate with both of these functions, their simultaneous exponential decay will minimize the negative effects of perturbation. Furthermore, it does not hinder convergence to a normal distribution.

Hence, we take
\varepsilon_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\beta_{n})
with density
\phi_{\beta_{n}}
, independent of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
. Here,
{\beta_{n}\in(0,1)}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\beta_{n}\to 0
in polynomial order for
n\to\infty
. The precise sequence
(\beta_{n})
will be fixed later.

Next, we derive the distribution of
T_{n}^{\prime}
in the conditional setting. To this end, let
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
and
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\geq 0
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(T_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}\in B\;|\;|X_{1}|=a_{1},\dots,|X_{n}|=a_{n}\big)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}\Big\{T_{n}=\tfrac{\sum\pm_{i}a_{j}}{(\sum a_{j}^{2})^{1/2}}\Big\}\mathbbm{1}\Big\{\varepsilon_{n}\in B-\tfrac{\sum\pm_{i}a_{j}}{(\sum a_{j}^{2})^{1/2}}\Big\}\;\Big|\;|X_{1}|=a_{1},\dots,|X_{n}|=a_{n}\Big].

As
T_{n}
and
\varepsilon_{n}
are independent given
|X_{1}|,\dots,|X_{n}|
, the expression above is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(T_{n}=\tfrac{\sum\pm_{i}a_{j}}{(\sum a_{j}^{2})^{1/2}}\;\Big|\;|X_{1}|=a_{1},\dots,|X_{n}|=a_{n}\Big)\mathbb{P}\Big(\varepsilon_{n}\in B-\tfrac{\sum\pm_{i}a_{j}}{(\sum a_{j}^{2})^{1/2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{B}2^{-n}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\phi_{\beta_{n}}\Big(y-\tfrac{\sum\pm_{i}a_{j}}{(\sum a_{j}^{2})^{1/2}}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}y.

Thus, the conditional distribution of
T_{n}^{\prime}=T_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}
is a convolution of the two underlying distributions, which will be denoted by
\ast
. Its conditional density has the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x):=f_{T_{n}^{\prime}\mid|X_{1}|,\dots,|X_{n}|}(x\mid|X_{1}|,\dots,|X_{n}|)=\widetilde{f}_{n}\ast\phi_{\beta_{n}}(x)=2^{-n}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\phi_{\beta_{n}}\Big(x-\tfrac{\sum\pm_{i}|X_{j}|}{V_{n}}\Big).

For the following steps until
4.3
, assume that the self-normalized sums
T_{n}
have densities
f_{n}
for
n\geq N
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and that we are in the regime
n\geq N
. This assumption is implied by the conditions of
Theorem

4.1
(see
Section

4.2
).

In the unconditional setting,
T_{n}
and
\varepsilon_{n}
are independent, too. Thus, the distribution of
T_{n}^{\prime}=T_{n}+\varepsilon_{n}
has the density

f_{n}^{\prime}(x)=f_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(x)=f_{n}\ast\phi_{\beta_{n}}(x)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f_{n}(x-y)\phi_{\beta_{n}}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y.

(4.2)

By Fubini’s theorem,
\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}
and
f_{n}^{\prime}
are connected by

\displaystyle\int_{B}\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)\big]\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{B}\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big]=\mathbb{P}\big(T_{n}^{\prime}\in B\big)=\int_{B}f_{n}^{\prime}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

for all
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
and thus
\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)\big]=f_{n}^{\prime}(x)

\lambda
-a.e. Since
\phi_{\beta_{n}}
is continuous,
\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}
is also a continuous function. If
x_{k}\to x
for
k\to\infty
, then

\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x_{k})\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\lim_{k\to\infty}\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x_{k})\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)\big]

by dominated convergence which is applicable since
0\leq\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)\leq(2\pi\beta_{n})^{-1/2}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}\big]
is also continuous. As
f_{n}\in L^{1}
and
\phi_{\beta_{n}}\in L^{\infty}
, the convolution
f_{n}^{\prime}=f_{n}\ast\phi_{\beta_{n}}
also is a bounded and continuous function (see e.g.
[
41
, Theorem 15.8]
). We can therefore conclude that

\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)\big]=f_{n}^{\prime}(x)\qquad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}.

(4.3)

For
T_{n}^{\prime}
, we proceed as described above. First, we need a version of
Proposition

3.2
that is suitable for
T_{n}^{\prime}
. We do not need perturbed approximation functions because we will choose
\beta_{n}
sufficiently small such that the approximation functions
\widetilde{p}
and
\widetilde{U}
are still appropriate. Recall the definitions
M_{n}=\max|X_{j}|
,
B_{n}=V_{n}/M_{n}
,
\widetilde{L}_{k,n}=V_{n}^{-k}\sum|X_{j}|^{k}
and

\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)=e^{-t^{2}/2}\Big(1+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)\Big),

where

\widetilde{U}_{r,n}(it)=\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}(it)^{r+2u(k_{\cdot})}\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}

from
2.6
and
2.7
, and where the sum
\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}
is introduced in
Section

2
. The following lemma is a consequence of
Proposition

3.2
. Its proof is deferred to
Section

A.3
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric,
V_{n}^{2}>0
and
m\geq 2
is an integer. Then

\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\big)\Big|\leq c(m)\big(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+\beta_{n}\big)e^{-t^{2}/6}\big(1+|t|^{3m+1+k}\big)

(4.4)

holds for
k=0,\dots,m
in the interval
|t|<B_{n}
.

Next, we prove the non-uniform bound for our Edgeworth expansion of the density of the perturbed distribution in the conditional setting.
Recall the definition
\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)=\phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-2}\widetilde{p}_{r,n}(x)
, where

\widetilde{p}_{r,n}(x)=\phi(x)\sum_{*(k_{\cdot},r,\cdot)}H_{r+2u(k_{\cdot})}(x)\prod_{l=1}^{r}\frac{1}{k_{l}!}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{l+2,n}}{(l+2)!}\Big)^{k_{l}}

from
2.13
and
2.14
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric,
V_{n}^{2}>0
and
m\geq 2
is an integer. Then

\begin{split}&\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)|\\
&\kern 5.0pt\leq c(m)\Big(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+\beta_{n}+J_{0,2}+J_{m,2}+\beta_{n}^{-(m+2)/2}n^{m-\log(n)/4}+e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}\Big),\end{split}

(4.5)

where
J_{k,2}=\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t
for
k=0,\dots,m
and
\nu_{n}=\beta_{n}^{-1/2}\cdot\log(n)
.

By conditional independence and
3.2
, the characteristic function of
T_{n}^{\prime}
has the form

\begin{split}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(t)&=\Big(\prod\widetilde{\varphi}_{X_{j}}(tV_{n}^{-1})\Big)\cdot\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}t^{2})\\
&=\Big(\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big)\cdot\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}t^{2}).\end{split}

(4.6)

For
k=0,\dots,m
, all derivatives
{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}
exist and are continuous. Furthermore,
{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}
is bounded by
c(m)n^{k}|t|^{k}\exp(-\beta_{n}t^{2}/2)\in L^{1}
and thus in
L^{1}
. The Fourier transform of our approximating function
\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)
also possesses these properties by
2.17
. This means that the conditions of the Fourier inversion theorem (see e.g.
[
8
, Theorem 4.1 (iv,v)]
) are satisfied. This yields

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|x|^{k}|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)|\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t

for
k=0,\dots,m
. As before, we split up the integral at
B_{n}
and additionally at
\nu_{n}
. This leads to

\begin{split}\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|x|^{k}|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)|$\mbox{}\hfil\quad\\
&\leq\underbrace{\int_{|t|<B_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(t)-\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=J_{k,1}}\,+\underbrace{\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=J_{k,2}}\\
&\quad+\underbrace{\int_{|t|\geq\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=J_{k,3}}\,+\underbrace{\int_{|t|\geq B_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{=J_{k,4}}.\end{split}

(4.7)

From
Lemma

4.4
we deduce

J_{k,1}\leq\int_{|t|<B_{n}}c(m)(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+\beta_{n}\big)e^{-t^{2}/6}\big(1+|t|^{3m+1+k}\big)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq c(m)(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+\beta_{n}\big),

for all
k=0,\dots,m
.

Next, by the Leibniz rule,

\begin{split}J_{k,3}&=\int_{|t|\geq\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\Big(\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big)\cdot\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}t^{2})\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leq\int_{|t|\geq\nu_{n}}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\binom{k}{l}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k-l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k-l}}}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}t^{2})\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t.\end{split}

(4.8)

As all derivatives of
\cos(x)
are bounded in absolute value by 1,

\displaystyle\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\prod\cos(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{n}=l}{l\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\prod\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{i_{j}}}{\mathrm{d}t^{i_{j}}}}\cos(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{n}=l}{l\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\prod\Big|(V_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|)^{i_{j}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{n}=l}{l\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}=n^{l}.

Here, the sum extends over all
n
-tuples
(i_{1},...,i_{n})
of non-negative integers with
\sum_{j=1}^{n}i_{j}=l
and

{l\choose i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}={\frac{l!}{i_{1}!\,i_{2}!\cdots i_{n}!}}.

Since
\beta_{n}<1
and
|t|\geq\nu_{n}\geq 1
, the
l
-th derivative of the exponential term in
4.8
is bounded by
c(k)|t|^{k}\exp(-\beta_{n}t^{2}/2)
for
l=0,\dots,k
.

Using
Lemma

A.4
,
J_{k,3}
can be bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{|t|\geq\nu_{n}}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\binom{k}{l}n^{l}\,c(k)|t|^{k}\exp(-\beta_{n}t^{2}/2)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq c(k)n^{k}\beta_{n}^{-(k+2)/2}\exp(-\beta_{n}\nu_{n}^{2}/4)

\displaystyle\leq c(m)n^{m}\beta_{n}^{-(m+2)/2}\exp\big(-(\log n)^{2}/4\big)

\displaystyle=c(m)\beta_{n}^{-(m+2)/2}n^{m-\log(n)/4}

for all
k=0,\dots,m
.

As in
3.28
, by
2.17
and since
|t|\geq B_{n}\geq 1
,

J_{k,4}=\int_{|t|\geq B_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{\Phi^{\widetilde{P}}_{m,n}}(t)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t\leq c(m)e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}

for all
k=0,\dots,m
, where we use
Lemma

A.4
(with
\beta=1
,
\nu_{n}=B_{n}
) in the last step.

Setting
k=0
and
k=m
in
4.7
now yields
4.5
.
∎

4.2
The unconditional setting

In the next step towards
Theorem

4.1
, we apply the expectation to the result of
Proposition

4.5
and prove the non-uniform bound for our Edgeworth expansion of the density of the perturbed distribution in the unconditional setting. For this purpose, we introduce the following condition
2
.
Together with a moment condition on
X_{1}
,
1
implies
2
by
Theorem

4.1
.

(C2)

There exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that the statistics
T_{n}
have densities
f_{n}

for
all
n\geq N
.

The well-known argument, which allows to conclude that the existence of a density
f_{N}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
implies the existence of densities
f_{n}
for any
n\geq N
which holds for the CLT-statistic
Z_{n}
does not apply here.
For both statistics
T_{n}
and
Z_{n}
, conditions of this type do not imply that
X_{1}
is absolutely continuous or even has a non-zero absolutely continuous component. For a counterexample with
X_{1},X_{2}
having singular continuous distribution and
X_{1}+X_{2}
being absolutely continuous with bounded density, see
[
37
, p. 17]
.

Note that in contrast to the distribution functions in the proof of
Theorem

1.1
, the densities
f_{n}
do not exist a priori. Therefore, the requirement
2
is crucial for applying
4.3
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{s}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
. Additionally assume that
2
is satisfied and
{\beta_{n}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)}
in polynomial order. Then for
m=\lfloor{s}\rfloor
,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+2m)/2}\big).

(4.9)

Let
n\geq N
with
N
from
2
. Recall
|T_{n}|\leq\sqrt{n}
from
Remark

3.10
and thus,
f_{n}(x)=0
for all
|x|>\sqrt{n}
.
Let
x>2\sqrt{n}
, then

\displaystyle(1+|x|)^{m}f_{n}^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=(1+x)^{m}\int_{-\sqrt{n}}^{\sqrt{n}}f_{n}(y)\phi_{\beta_{n}}(x-y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq(1+x)^{m}\phi_{\beta_{n}}(x-\sqrt{n}\,)\int_{-\sqrt{n}}^{\sqrt{n}}f_{n}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=(1+x)^{m}(2\pi\beta_{n})^{-1/2}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}^{-1}(x-\sqrt{n}\,)^{2}\big).

Due to

\displaystyle{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}}(1+x)^{m}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}^{-1}(x-\sqrt{n}\,)^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\Big(m-(1+x)\beta_{n}^{-1}(x-\sqrt{n}\,)\Big)(1+x)^{m-1}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}^{-1}(x-\sqrt{n}\,)^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\Big(m-(1+2\sqrt{n}\,)\beta_{n}^{-1}\sqrt{n}\Big)(1+x)^{m-1}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}^{-1}(x-\sqrt{n}\,)^{2}\big)<0,

(1+x)^{m}\exp\big(-\beta_{n}^{-1}(x-\sqrt{n}\,)^{2}/2\big)
is monotonically decreasing for
x>2\sqrt{n}
(and
n>m
). Therefore,

\begin{split}\sup_{x>2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}f_{n}^{\prime}(x)&\leq\sup_{x>2\sqrt{n}}(1+x)^{m}(2\pi\beta_{n})^{-1/2}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}^{-1}(x-\sqrt{n}\,)^{2}\big)\\
&=(1+2\sqrt{n}\,)^{m}(2\pi\beta_{n})^{-1/2}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}^{-1}n\big)=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)\end{split}

(4.10)

in particular.
This bound also holds for negative
x
as
f_{n}
and
\phi_{\beta_{n}}
are symmetric. Additionally, due to the form
1.5
and the factor
\phi
in all expansion terms
q_{r}
,

\sup_{|x|>2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\big|\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)\big|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

(4.11)

Therefore, the left-hand side of
4.9
is of order
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)
for the supremum restricted to
|x|>2\sqrt{n}
. That is why we only have to consider the supremum over
|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}
throughout the remainder of this proof.

In order to apply
Proposition

4.5
, the left-hand side of
4.9
has to be converted into a form related to the left-hand side of
4.5
. By
4.3
, Jensen’s inequality and
Proposition

2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\right]\right|+\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\right]-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\right|\right]+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

Due to the form of
\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}
and uniform boundedness of
\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}
(see
2.14
and
2.19
),

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|2^{-n}\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\phi_{\beta_{n}}\Big(x-\tfrac{\sum\pm_{i}|X_{j}|}{V_{n}}\Big)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(2\sqrt{n}\,)^{m}\left((2\pi\beta_{n})^{-1/2}+c(m)\right)=\mathcal{O}\big(n^{m/2}\beta_{n}^{-1/2}\big).

By
3.29
for
\tau_{n}:=n^{(m+s)/(2s)}\beta_{n}^{-1/(2s)}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(M_{n}\geq\tau_{n})=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m+s-2)/2}\beta_{n}^{1/2}\big).

Let
\zeta>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(|X_{1}|\leq\zeta)<1
. Then,

n^{m/2}\beta_{n}^{-1/2}\cdot\mathbb{P}(M_{n}\leq\zeta)=n^{m/2}\beta_{n}^{-1/2}\cdot\mathbb{P}(|X_{1}|\leq\zeta)^{n}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)

in particular. Therefore, decomposing into
\{M_{n}\leq\zeta\},\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}
and
\{M_{n}\geq\tau_{n}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\right|\right]+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

Recall
B_{n}=V_{n}/M_{n}
. Applying
Proposition

4.5
(
M_{n}>\zeta
guarantees
V_{n}^{2}>0
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\left|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{\widetilde{p}}_{m,n}(x)\right|\right]\leq c(m)\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Big(\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}+\beta_{n}+J_{0,2}+J_{m,2}+\beta_{n}^{-(m+2)/2}n^{m-\log(n)/4}+e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}\Big)\Big],

where
J_{k,2}=\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k}}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{T_{n}^{\prime}}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t
for
k=0,\dots,m
and
\nu_{n}=\beta_{n}^{-1/2}\cdot\log(n)
.

For the first summand,

\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{L}_{m+1,n}]=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

by
Lemma

3.8
with
m+1>{s}
.
Clearly, for the second summand
\beta_{n}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)
and for the fifth summand
\beta_{n}^{-(m+2)/2}n^{m-\log(n)/4}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)
as
\beta_{n}
decreases in polynomial order and
n^{-\log(n)}
is smaller than
n^{-k}
for every
k>0
and
n
sufficiently large.

Now we examine the expectation of
J_{k,2}
. Notice
\beta_{n}|t|<\beta_{n}\nu_{n}=\sqrt{\beta_{n}}\cdot\log(n)\leq 1
in the domain of the integral for
n
sufficiently large.
For
B_{n}\leq t<\nu_{n}
and
a\geq 0
, this yields

\displaystyle\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{a}}{\mathrm{d}t^{a}}}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}t^{2})\Big|\leq c(a)\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}t^{2})\leq c(a).

Thus by
4.6
and the Leibniz formula,

\displaystyle J_{k,2}

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\Big|\sum_{l=0}^{k}\binom{k}{l}\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big)\Big({\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k-l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k-l}}}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}t^{2})\Big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq c(k)\sum_{l=0}^{k}\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k-l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{k-l}}}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}\beta_{n}t^{2})\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq c(k)\sum_{l=0}^{k}\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t.

As
\nu_{n}=\beta_{n}^{-1/2}\cdot\log(n)
and
\tau_{n}=n^{(m+s)/(2s)}\beta_{n}^{-1/(2s)}
with
\beta_{n}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)
grow in polynomial order, by
Proposition

3.5
we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}J_{k,2}\big]

\displaystyle\leq c(k)\sum_{l=0}^{k}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\zeta<M_{n}<\tau_{n}\}}\int_{B_{n}\leq|t|<\nu_{n}}\Big|{\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}}\prod\cos\big(tV_{n}^{-1}|X_{j}|\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}t\bigg]

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+\max\{2k,1\})/2}\big)

for
k=0,\dots,m
. Regarding the sixth summand,
Lemma

3.9
yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-B_{n}^{2}/4}\right]=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{{s}/2}\big).

Therefore
4.9
holds and
Proposition

4.6
is proved.
∎

Proposition

4.6
provides a non-uniform bound for
f_{n}^{\prime}
. We will now deconvolute the densities to achieve a similar bound for
f_{n}
.
The Hölder type condition
(iii)
of the following result seems hard to verify, but it will turn out to be satisfied by the conditions of
Theorem

4.1
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Assume that the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

X_{1}
is symmetric, the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular, and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{s}<\infty
for some
{s}\geq 2
.

(ii)

2
is satisfied and there exists
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that there exist versions of
f_{n}

that are bounded uniformly over all
n\geq N_{1}
.

(iii)

There exist
a\in(0,1],b,\alpha>0
(that are allowed to depend on
s
),
N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence
(r_{n})
with
r_{n}\to 0
such that for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leq m\leq{s}
,

(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-h)|\leq\alpha\,(1+|x|)^{m}|h|^{a}n^{b}+r_{n}

(4.12)

holds for all
n\geq N_{2}
,
|h|<1
and for all
|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}
.

Then,

\begin{split}&\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|\\
&\quad=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+2\lfloor{s}\rfloor)/2}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-\lceil\frac{m-1}{2}\rceil}\big)+r_{n}.\end{split}

(4.13)

Let
n\geq N\vee N_{1}\vee N_{2}
with
N
from
2
. In this proof we decompose the left-hand side of
4.13
such that

\begin{split}\lx@add@cssclass{ltx_eqn_lefteqn}$\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|$\mbox{}\hfil\quad\\
&\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}^{\prime}(x)|+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{\lfloor{s}\rfloor}|f_{n}^{\prime}(x)-\phi^{q}_{\lfloor s\rfloor,n}(x)|\\
&\quad+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|\phi^{q}_{\lfloor{s}\rfloor,n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|.\end{split}

(4.14)

Now we fix
\beta_{n}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(2b+m+{s}-2)/a}\big)
(polynomial in
n
), which satisfies the assumptions of
Proposition

4.6
. Thus, the second summand of the right-hand side in
4.14
is of order
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+2\lfloor{s}\rfloor)/2}\big)
.

By
1.5
, the third summand equals

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}\Big|\sum_{r=m-1}^{\lfloor{s}\rfloor-2}q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}\Big|\leq c({s})n^{-\lceil\frac{m-1}{2}\rceil}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Similar to
4.11
, the bound is due to the factor
\phi
in all expansion terms
q_{r}
and the fact
q_{r}=0
for uneven
r
.

As a last step, we need to bound
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}^{\prime}(x)|
. By
Remark

3.10
,
|T_{n}|\leq\sqrt{n}
and thus
f_{n}(x)=0
for all
|x|>\sqrt{n}
. Then,
4.10
implies

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|>2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}^{\prime}(x)|=\sup_{|x|>2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}f_{n}^{\prime}(x)\leq\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big).

Hence, from now on we only have to consider the supremum over
|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}
. For
{\varepsilon_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\beta_{n})}
with density
\phi_{\beta_{n}}
, we get

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}^{\prime}(x)|

\displaystyle=\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\Big|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\big(f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-y)\big)\phi_{\beta_{n}}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\Big|

(4.15)

\displaystyle=\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\Big|\mathbb{E}\big[f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-\varepsilon_{n})\big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\big|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-\varepsilon_{n})\big|\Big].

By
4.12
, we get for
|\varepsilon_{n}|<1

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-\varepsilon_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}\alpha\,(1+|x|)^{m}|\varepsilon_{n}|^{a}n^{b}+r_{n}

\displaystyle=c(s)\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|\varepsilon_{n}|^{a}n^{b}+r_{n}.

By uniform boundedness of
f_{n}
for
|\varepsilon_{n}|\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\big|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-\varepsilon_{n})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\cdot 2\sup_{n\geq N_{1}}\|f_{n}\|_{\sup}

\displaystyle\leq c(s)\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|\varepsilon_{n}|^{a}n^{b}+r_{n}.

Therefore, noting that
\varepsilon_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\beta_{n})
, we deduce from
4.2

\displaystyle\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}^{\prime}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}\big|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-\varepsilon_{n})\big|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[c(s)\sup_{|x|\leq 2\sqrt{n}}(1+|x|)^{m}|\varepsilon_{n}|^{a}n^{b}+r_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq c(s)n^{(2b+m)/2}\big(\mathbb{E}\varepsilon_{n}^{2}\big)^{a/2}+r_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\big)+r_{n}.

∎

4.3
The smooth function model

In this subsection, we briefly explain the implications of the smooth function model for self-normalized sums, which we use in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
. For a more detailed introduction, see
[
30
, Sections 2.4 and 2.8]
. Let
Y_{j}:=(X_{j},X_{j}^{2})
with mean
\mu_{Y}=(0,1)
and covariance matrix

\displaystyle\Sigma:=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(Y)=\begin{pmatrix}1&\mu_{3}\\
\mu_{3}&\mu_{4}-1\end{pmatrix}.

Assume that
1
holds and that
\mu_{4}<\infty
. This implies that
(X_{1},X_{1}^{2})
is non-degenerate and thus
\Sigma
is positive definite, see
Remark

A.2
. Let
Y^{(i)}
denote the
i
-th coordinate of
Y
and
\overline{Y}:=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum Y_{j}
.

If
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m}<\infty
holds for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 3
, by
[
8
, Theorem 19.2]
, for
n
sufficiently large, the density of
n^{1/2}(\overline{Y}-\mu_{Y})
,
g_{n}
exists, is bounded and admits the expansion

\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}(1+\|y\|)^{m}\Big|g_{n}(y)-\sum_{r=0}^{m-2}n^{-r/2}\pi_{r}(y)\phi_{\Sigma}(y)\Big|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}\big),

(4.16)

where
\pi_{r}
is a bivariate polynomial of degree
3r
(which includes moments up to order
2r+4
) and
\phi_{\Sigma}
denotes the 2-dimensional normal density with parameters 0 and
\Sigma
.

Next, we derive a density
f_{n}
of
T_{n}
in terms of
g_{n}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T_{n}\leq a)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(n^{1/2}\,\overline{Y^{(1)}}\,\Big(\overline{Y^{(2)}}\Big)^{-1/2}\leq a\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\bigg(\Big(n^{1/2}\,\overline{Y^{(1)}}\Big)\,\Big(n^{-1/2}n^{1/2}\Big(\overline{Y^{(2)}}-1\Big)+1\Big)^{-1/2}\leq a\bigg)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathbbm{1}\Big\{u_{1}\,\big(n^{-1/2}u_{2}+1\big)^{-1/2}\leq a,\,u_{2}>-n^{1/2}\Big\}g_{n}(u)\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\int_{-n^{1/2}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{a\,(n^{-1/2}u_{2}+1)^{1/2}}g_{n}(u_{1},u_{2})\,\mathrm{d}u_{1}\,\mathrm{d}u_{2}.

By substituting
u_{1}=x\big(n^{-1/2}u_{2}+1\big)^{1/2}
, applying Fubini’s theorem, and substituting
{u_{2}=z-n^{1/2}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T_{n}\leq a)

\displaystyle=\int_{-n^{1/2}}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{a}g_{n}\big(x\,(n^{-1/2}u_{2}+1)^{1/2},u_{2}\big)\big(n^{-1/2}u_{2}+1\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}u_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{a}\int_{-n^{1/2}}^{\infty}g_{n}\big(x\,(n^{-1/2}u_{2}+1)^{1/2},u_{2}\big)\big(n^{-1/2}u_{2}+1\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}u_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{a}\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{n}\big(x\,(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{a}\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{n}\big(\gamma_{x,n}(z)\big)\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}x

where
\gamma_{x,n}(z)=\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)
and thus

\displaystyle f_{n}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{n}\big(\gamma_{x,n}(z)\big)\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z.

(4.17)

Remark 4.8
.

In this context, Equation (2.67) on page 79 in
[
30
]
states that

f_{n}(x)=\int_{S(n,x)}g_{n}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

(4.18)

with

S(n,x):=\big\{\gamma_{x,n}(z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\colon z\in(0,\infty)\big\}
.
However, the integral in
4.18
can neither be understood as an integral with respect to the two-dimensional Lebesgue measure (as it vanishes in this case) nor as a classical curve integral for the parametrization of
S(n,x)
by
z\mapsto\gamma_{x,n}(z)=\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)
, namely

\displaystyle\int_{S(n,x)}g_{n}\,=\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{n}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\|\dot{\gamma}_{x,n}(z)\|\,\mathrm{d}z.

Indeed, adopting the latter strategy for the uniform distribution on the lower triangle of the unit square

\bigcup_{x\in[0,1]}S(x)

with

\displaystyle S(x)=\big\{\gamma_{x,n}(t)\in[0,1]\times[0,1]\colon t\in[0,1-x]\big\}\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{x,n}(t)=(x+t,t),

we do not obtain a density:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\int_{S(x)}2\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1-x}2\,\|\dot{\gamma}_{x,n}(t)\|\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{2}\neq 1.

4.4
Proof of Theorem
4.1

In the following remark, we bound polynomials that appear in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

Remark 4.9
.

In the following derivations, we will often consider polynomials of the form
\pi_{r}\big(\gamma_{x,n}(z)\big)
, where
\pi_{r}
is a bivariate polynomial of degree
3r
, which includes moments of
X_{1}
up to order
(2r+4)
and
\gamma_{x,n}(z)=\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)
. Integration of these polynomials leads to cumbersome calculations which we shall avoid by the subsequent upper bounds.
Let
\pi_{r,1}
denote the function which arises from
\pi_{r}
by replacing all coefficients and both arguments by their absolute values. Then

\big|\pi_{r}\big(\gamma_{x,n}(z)\big)\big|=\big|\pi_{r}\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)\big|\leq\pi_{r,1}\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big).

Now,
\pi_{r,1}
is symmetric in both arguments and thus non-decreasing for growing absolute values of the variables. Hence, replacing the factors
n^{-1/4}
by 1 and
|x|
by
(|x|\vee 1)
yields an upper bound. This yields

\displaystyle\pi_{r,1}\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\pi_{r,1}\big((|x|\vee 1)z^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq(|x|\vee 1)^{3r}\pi_{r,1}\big(z+1,z-n^{1/2}\big)

because the degree of the polynomial is at most
3r
. Next,

\pi_{r,1}\big(z+1,z-n^{1/2}\big)\leq c(r)n^{3r/2}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(|z|)

for some suitable univariate polynomial
\widehat{\pi}_{r}
of degree
3r
.
If
|x|<2\sqrt{n}
, we have

\big|\pi_{r}\big(\gamma_{x,n}(z)\big)\big|\leq c(r)n^{3r/2}(|x|\vee 1)^{3r}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(|z|)\leq c(r)n^{3r}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(|z|).

Now we are in the position to prove the main theorem.

Depending on which moment assumption in
Theorem

4.1
is satisfied, let
{w}=m
or
{w}=m+1
. In any case,
{w}\in\mathbb{N}
,
{w}\geq 3
and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2{w}}<\infty
.

1
and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2{w}}<\infty
yield that there exists
N_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
4.17
and
4.16
hold for all
n\geq N_{1}
. Throughout the remainder of the proof assume
n\geq N_{1}
. Now
4.17
implies
2
. In order to apply
Proposition

4.7
, it remains to show that
f_{n}
are uniformly bounded over
n\geq N_{1}
and that the Hölder-type condition
(iii)
holds. We start with the latter.

4.17
and
4.16
together with the required notation as introduced in
Section

4.3
yield

\begin{split}f_{n}(x)&=\int_{0}^{\infty}g_{n}\big(\gamma_{x,n}(z)\big)\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\phi_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\\
&\qquad\qquad+\big(1+\|\gamma_{x,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}R_{n}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\Big)\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z,\end{split}

(4.19)

where
\pi_{r}
is a bivariate polynomial of degree
3r
, which includes moments up to order
2r+4
and
\phi_{\Sigma}
denotes the 2-dimensional normal density with parameters 0 and

\displaystyle\Sigma=\begin{pmatrix}1\enskip&0\\
0\enskip&\mu_{4}-1\end{pmatrix}.

1
implies that
X_{1}^{2}
is non-degenerate and thus
\Sigma
is positive definite. Additionally,
\gamma_{x,n}(z)=\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)
and

R_{n}^{\prime}:=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}|R_{n}(y)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({w}-2)/2}\big).

(4.20)

Then by the triangle-inequality,

\displaystyle|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-h)|

(4.21)

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\phi_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big(1+\|\gamma_{x,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}R_{n}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad-\Big(\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x-h,n}(z))\phi_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{x-h,n}(z))\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big(1+\|\gamma_{x-h,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}R_{n}(\gamma_{x-h,n}(z))\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}z\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))-\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x-h,n}(z))\big|\,\phi_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\,\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x-h,n}(z))\big|\,\big|\phi_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))-\phi_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{x-h,n}(z))\big|\,\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad+R_{n}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1+\|\gamma_{x,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}+\big(1+\|\gamma_{x-h,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=:K_{1}+K_{2}+K_{3}.

Recall that we are in the case of
|x|<2\sqrt{n}
,
|h|<1
. All following formulas will be deduced uniformly in this regime.

Remark

4.9
provides the bound

\big|\pi_{r}\big(\gamma_{x,n}(z)\big)\big|\leq c(r)n^{3r}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(|z|)

for
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Here
\widehat{\pi}_{r}
being a polynomial of degree
3r
whose coefficients depend on the coefficients of
\pi_{r}
and
r
. Due to

\begin{split}\big|\pi_{r}\big(\gamma_{x-h,n}(z)\big)\big|\leq c(r)n^{3r/2}(|x|+1)^{3r}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(|z|)\leq c(r)n^{3r}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(|z|),\end{split}

the bound also holds if we replace
x
by
x-h
.
Note that

\displaystyle\phi_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))=\phi\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}\big)\,\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big).

We also need to bound integrals of the form

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(z)\,z\,\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(z)\,|z|\,\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\widehat{\pi}_{r}(W_{n})|W_{n}|\Big],

where
W_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(n^{1/2},\mu_{4}-1)
is a normally distributed random variable with mean
n^{1/2}
and variance
\mu_{4}-1
. As the expected value is bounded up to a constant by the sum over the first
(3r+1)
absolute moments of
W_{n}
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(z)\,z\,\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}z\leq c(r)n^{(3r+1)/2}.

(4.22)

Now we expand

\displaystyle K_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}\big)\,\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Big|\pi_{r}\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)-\pi_{r}\big((x-h)(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}z.

The polynomial
\pi_{r}\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)
is a part of
\pi_{r}\big((x-h)(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},\allowbreak z-n^{1/2}\big)
in the sense that every summand of the first polynomial is also a summand of the second polynomial. The remaining summands of
\pi_{r}\big((x-h)(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)
all feature the factor
h(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}
. Therefore,
h(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}
is a common factor of every summand of
\pi_{r}\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},\allowbreak z-n^{1/2}\big)-\pi_{r}\big((x-h)(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)
and thus

\displaystyle\big|\pi_{r}\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)-\pi_{r}\big((x-h)(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2},z-n^{1/2}\big)\big|

\displaystyle\quad\leq|h|(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}\cdot\pi_{r,2}\big((|x|\vee|x-h|\vee 1)(n^{-1/2}|z|)^{1/2},|z-n^{1/2}|\big).

for a suitable polynomial
\pi_{r,2}
with non-negative coefficients and degree at most
3r
.
Similar in spirit to
Remark

4.9
, we bound

\pi_{r,2}\big((|x|\vee|x-h|\vee 1)(n^{-1/2}|z|)^{1/2},|z-n^{1/2}|\big)\leq c(r)n^{3r}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(z)

with
\widehat{\pi}_{r}
being a polynomial of degree
3r
whose coefficients depend on the coefficients of
\pi_{r}
and
r
. Using
4.22
, the expression above is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\cdot|h|(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}c(r)n^{3r}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq|h|c(w)\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{(5r-1)/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,z\,\widehat{\pi}_{r}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq|h|c({w})\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{(5r-1)/2}n^{(3r+1)/2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq|h|c({w})n^{4{w}-8}.

We conclude that

K_{1}\leq|h|c({w})n^{4{w}-8}.

(4.23)

For
K_{2}
, we use that
\phi
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
L>0
and get

\displaystyle K_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big|\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x-h,n}(z))\big|\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\cdot\Big|\phi\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}\big)-\phi\big((x-h)(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}\big)\Big|\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}c(r)n^{3r}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(z)\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)

(4.24)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\cdot L|h|(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq|h|c({w})\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{(5r-1)/2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\widehat{\pi}_{r}(z)\,z\,\phi_{\mu_{4}-1}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq|h|c({w})n^{4{w}-8}.

We are left with

K_{3}=R_{n}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1+\|\gamma_{x,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}+\big(1+\|\gamma_{x-h,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

where it is obviously impossible to attain a bound in
h
. Therefore, we also have to take the factor
(1+|x|)^{m}
from
4.12
into account and find a bound in
n
. We only examine the first summand as the second one can be handled in a similar fashion. Thus,

\displaystyle(1+|x|)^{m}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1+\|\gamma_{x,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq c(m)\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{(1+x^{2})^{m}\big(1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad=c(m)\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1+x^{2}}{1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}}\bigg)^{m}}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\quad\cdot\sqrt{\big(1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-({w}-m)}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)}\,\mathrm{d}z.

Recalling
w\geq m
and
|x|<2\sqrt{n}
, we split this integral into three regions that we treat separately. First,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\sqrt{n}/2}\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1+x^{2}}{1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}}\bigg)^{m}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\cdot\sqrt{\big(1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-({w}-m)}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{0}^{\sqrt{n}/2}\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1+x^{2}}{(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}-n^{1/2})^{2}}\bigg)^{m}\big((\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-({w}-m)}\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{0}^{\sqrt{n}/2}\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1+4n}{n/4}\bigg)^{m}\big(n/4\big)^{-({w}-m)}}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq c(w)n^{(m+1-{w})/2}

and second by
Lemma

A.6
,

\displaystyle\int_{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}}^{2\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1+x^{2}}{1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}}\bigg)^{m}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\cdot\sqrt{\big(1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-({w}-m)}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}}^{2\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1+x^{2}}{1+x^{2}/2}\bigg)^{m}\big(1+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-({w}-m)}\cdot 2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq c(m)\int_{\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{n}}^{2\sqrt{n}}\big(1+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-({w}-m)/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq\begin{cases}c(w)\sqrt{n},&\text{ if }{w}=m,\\
c(w)\log n,&\text{ if }{w}=m+1.\end{cases}

Finally,

\displaystyle\int_{2\sqrt{n}}^{\infty}\sqrt{\bigg(\frac{1+x^{2}}{1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}}\bigg)^{m}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\cdot\sqrt{\big(1+n^{-1/2}zx^{2}+(z-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-({w}-m)}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq\int_{2\sqrt{n}}^{\infty}\sqrt{(1+4n)^{m}\big((z-n^{1/2})^{2}\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq c(m)n^{m/2}n^{-1/4}\int_{2\sqrt{n}}^{\infty}\sqrt{\big((z-z/2)^{2}\big)^{-{w}}\cdot z}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq c(m)n^{m/2}n^{-1/4}\int_{2\sqrt{n}}^{\infty}z^{-(2{w}-1)/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad\leq c(w)n^{(m+1-{w})/2}.

This also holds if
x
is replaced by
x-h
and thus combining the last four equations and
4.20

\displaystyle(1+|x|)^{m}K_{3}

\displaystyle=(1+|x|)^{m}R_{n}^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big(1+\|\gamma_{x,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\big(1+\|\gamma_{x-h,n}(z)\|\big)^{-{w}}\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq R_{n}^{\prime}\begin{cases}c(w)\sqrt{n},&\text{ if }{w}=m,\\
c(w)\log n,&\text{ if }{w}=m+1,\end{cases}

(4.25)

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-3)/2}\big),&\text{ if }{w}=m,\\
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\,\log n\big),&\text{ if }{w}=m+1,\end{cases}

\displaystyle=:r_{n}

uniformly for all
|x|<2\sqrt{n}
,
|h|<1
. In summary, we get by
4.21
,
4.23
,
4.4
and
4.4
,

(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x-h)|\leq c(w)(1+|x|)^{m}|h|n^{4{w}-8}+r_{n}.

Hence, setting
a=1
and
b=4{w}-8
, the Hölder-type condition
(iii)
in
Proposition

4.7
is satisfied.
It remains to derive a uniform bound on
f_{n}
. For this aim we subsequently use the identity for the normal density

\displaystyle\phi_{\sigma^{2}}(x)=\phi_{\sigma^{2}/2}(x)\,\phi_{\sigma^{2}/2}(x)\sqrt{2/(\pi\sigma^{2})}

for
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma^{2}>0
.
By
4.4
and
4.19
, it suffices to bound

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\phi_{\Sigma}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2/(\pi(\mu_{4}-1))}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\,\phi\big(x(n^{-1/2}z)^{1/2}\big)\,\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2/(\pi(\mu_{4}-1))}\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{{w}-2}n^{-r/2}\pi_{r}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))\phi_{\Sigma_{2}}(\gamma_{x,n}(z))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,\big(n^{-1/2}z\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq c(w)n^{-1/4}\int_{0}^{\infty}\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,z^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

where

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1\enskip&0\\
0\enskip&(\mu_{4}-1)/2\end{pmatrix}.

The integral is divided into two parts that are evaluated separately. First,

\displaystyle c(w)n^{-1/4}\int_{0}^{2n^{1/2}}\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,z^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq c(w)\int_{0}^{2n^{1/2}}\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq c(w)

and second,

\displaystyle c(w)n^{-1/4}\int_{2n^{1/2}}^{\infty}\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}\big(z-n^{1/2}\big)\,z^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=c(w)n^{-1/4}\int_{n^{1/2}}^{\infty}\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}(z)\,\big(z+n^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq c(w)n^{-1/4}\int_{n^{1/2}}^{\infty}\phi_{(\mu_{4}-1)/2}(z)\,z^{2}\,\mathrm{d}z\leq c(w).

Thus,
f_{n}
is bounded uniformly over all
n\geq N_{1}
and
Proposition

4.7
yields

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-({s}-2)/2}\,(\log n)^{({s}+2\lfloor{s}\rfloor)/2}\big)+\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-\lceil\frac{m-1}{2}\rceil}\big)+r_{n}

for both
m=w-1
and
m=w
, and for all
{s}
with
m\leq{s}\leq 2{w}
. We choose
{s}=m+2
to finally get

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,(1+|x|)^{m}|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m,n}(x)|

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-3)/2}\big),&\text{ if }{w}=m,\\
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\,\log n\big),&\text{ if }{w}=m+1.\end{cases}

∎

5
Rate of convergence and Edgeworth-type expansion in the entropic central limit theorem for self-normalized sums

The main result of this section is the following theorem.

Theorem 5.1
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular,
1
is satisfied and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 3
. Then

D(T_{n})=\frac{c_{2}}{n^{2}}+\dots+\frac{c_{\lfloor(m-2)/2\rfloor}}{n^{\lfloor(m-2)/2\rfloor}}+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big((n\log n)^{-(m-2)/2}\,\log n\big),

(5.1)

where

\displaystyle c_{l}=\sum_{k=2}^{2l}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\sum\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{r_{1}}(x)\dots q_{r_{k}}(x)}{\phi(x)^{k-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad l\in\mathbb{N},

with the inner sum running over all positive integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
such that
r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}=2l
.

Together with
Proposition

4.2
,
Theorem

5.1
implies
Theorem

1.3
. Before starting with the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, let us record the following observation.

Remark 5.2
.

The classical statistic
Z_{n}
is normalized in the sense that
\mathbb{E}Z_{n}=0
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}Z_{n}=1
. The
t
-statistic is not normalized (see
[
30
, p. 72f.]
). In general, the self-normalized sum is also not normalized (see
Remark

A.3
). If
X_{1}
is symmetric however, it is normalized because

\mathbb{E}T_{n}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\big[S_{n}/V_{n}\big]\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[V_{n}^{-1}\sum\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\big[X_{j}\big]\Big]=0

and

\mathbb{E}T_{n}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\big[S_{n}^{2}/V_{n}^{2}\big]\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[V_{n}^{-2}\Big(\sum\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\big[X_{j}^{2}\big]+2\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\sum_{k=j+1}^{n}\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}\big[X_{j}X_{k}\big]\Big)\Big]=1.

In the following proof, we adapt some ideas from
[
13
]
.

By
Theorem

4.1
, the densities
f_{n}
exist and are uniformly bounded by some constant
M
for
n\geq N
. Throughout the remainder of the proof assume
n\geq N
.
Since
X_{1}
is symmetric,
T_{n}
is normalized (see
Remark

5.2
). Therefore, its relative entropy, defined in
1.7
, includes the standard normal density
\phi_{0,1}=\phi
.

Set
\Delta_{m,n}=(n\log n)^{-(m-2)/2}\,\log n
.
Let
A_{n}\geq 1
, then we split up the integral from the definition

\displaystyle D(T_{n})=\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\log\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{|x|>A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\log\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x.

(5.2)

For the second integral, we get the upper bound

\displaystyle\int_{|x|>A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\log\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x|>A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\log\frac{M}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\log\big(M\sqrt{2\pi}\big)\int_{|x|>A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{|x|>A_{n}}x^{2}f_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\log\big(M\sqrt{2\pi}\big)A_{n}^{-2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Big)\int_{|x|>A_{n}}x^{2}f_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

By means of the inequality
u\log u\geq u-1
for
u>0
, we get the lower bound

\displaystyle\int_{|x|>A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\log\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\phi(x)\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\log\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\phi(x)\Big(\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}-1\Big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\big(f_{n}(x)-\phi(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq-\mathbb{P}\{|Z|>A_{n}\}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{|x|>A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\log\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|\leq C\int_{|x|>A_{n}}x^{2}f_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathbb{P}\{|Z|>A_{n}\},

(5.3)

where
C=\log\big(M\sqrt{2\pi}\big)+1/2
. Before further evaluation of this term, we specify
A_{n}
with the intent to balance both integrals in
5.2
.
Set

\displaystyle A_{n}=\sqrt{(m-2)\log n+(m-3)\log\log n+\rho_{n}},

where
\rho_{n}\to\infty
sufficiently slow and
\rho_{n}\leq\log n
.
(\rho_{n})
is needed to achieve the order
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}
and not only
\mathcal{O}
in the remainder in
5.1
. It will be defined at a later stage, depending on the remainder in
Theorem

1.2
. For any
k\geq 0
, the bound

\begin{split}A_{n}^{k}\,e^{-A_{n}^{2}/2}&=\big((m-2)\log n+(m-3)\log\log n+\rho_{n}\big)^{k/2}\\
&\quad\cdot n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3)/2}\,e^{-\rho_{n}/2}\\
&=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3-k)/2}\big)\end{split}

(5.4)

is frequently needed.

Recall that
|T_{n}|\leq\sqrt{n}
by
Remark

3.10
. We return to
5.3
and derive by partial integration for Stieltjes integrals (see e.g.
[
32
, Theorem 21.67 (iv)]
) using the symmetry of
T_{n}
,

\displaystyle\int_{|x|>A_{n}}x^{2}f_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2\int_{A_{n}}^{\sqrt{n}}x^{2}\,\mathrm{d}F_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=2\Big(\sqrt{n}^{2}(F_{n}(\sqrt{n}\,))-A_{n}^{2}(F_{n}(A_{n}))-\int_{A_{n}}^{\sqrt{n}}F_{n}(x)2x\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)

(5.5)

\displaystyle=2A_{n}^{2}\big(1-F_{n}(A_{n})\big)+4\int_{A_{n}}^{\infty}x\big(1-F_{n}(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

By
Theorem

1.1
(with
s=m+1
),

F_{n}(x)=\Phi^{Q}_{m+1,n}(x)+(1+|x|)^{-(m+1)}R_{n}(x)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
n
sufficiently large. Here,
\sup_{x}|R_{n}(x)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\,(\log n)^{(3m+3)/2}\big)
.

Therefore, we can replace
A_{n}^{2}\big(1-F_{n}(A_{n})\big)
with

A_{n}^{2}\big(1-\Phi^{Q}_{m+1,n}(A_{n})\big)

with an error of magnitude

\displaystyle A_{n}^{2}(1+A_{n})^{-(m+1)}R_{n}(A_{n})=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\,(\log n)^{(2m+4)/2}\big).

Recall the definition

\displaystyle\Phi^{Q}_{m+1,n}(x)=\Phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}Q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}

from
1.1
. For uneven
r
,
Q_{r}
vanishes and for even
r
,
Q_{r}
has the useful form of
\phi
multiplied with a polynomial of degree
2r-1
. The coefficients of
Q_{r}
are functions of the moments
\mu_{3},\dots,\mu_{r+2}
and can thus be bounded by
c(r)
. Now by
5.4
,

\displaystyle\Big|A_{n}^{2}\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}Q_{r}(A_{n})n^{-r/2}\Big|\leq A_{n}^{2}\,c(m)\,A_{n}^{2m-3}\phi(A_{n})n^{-1/2}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}(\log n)^{(m+2)/2}\big).

For the last part, we use
1-\Phi(x)\leq\phi(x)/x
(for
x>0
) and
5.4
to achieve

\displaystyle A_{n}^{2}\big(1-\Phi(A_{n})\big)\leq A_{n}\phi(A_{n})=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n})

and therefore

\displaystyle A_{n}^{2}\big(1-F_{n}(A_{n})\big)=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n}).

Similarly, in the second part of
5
, we can replace
\int_{A_{n}}^{\infty}x\big(1-F_{n}(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x
with

\displaystyle\int_{A_{n}}^{\infty}x\big(1-\Phi^{Q}_{m+1,n}(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

with an error of at most

\displaystyle\int_{A_{n}}^{\infty}x(1+|x|)^{-(m+1)}R_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\,(\log n)^{(2m+4)/2}\big).

As above, using
\alpha=\frac{1}{2(2m-2)}
,
Lemma

A.4
(with
\beta=1
,
\nu_{n}=n^{\alpha}
) and
5.4
,

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{A_{n}}^{\infty}x\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}Q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|

\displaystyle\leq c(m)n^{-1/2}\Big(\int_{A_{n}}^{n^{\alpha}}x^{2m-2}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{n^{\alpha}}^{\infty}x^{2m-2}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big)

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\int_{A_{n}}^{n^{\alpha}}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+c(m)\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2\alpha}\big)

(5.6)

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\phi(A_{n})+c(m)\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{4}n^{2\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3)/2}\big)

and by
5.4
,

\displaystyle\int_{A_{n}}^{\infty}x\big(1-\Phi(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A_{n}}^{\infty}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\phi(A_{n})=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3)/2}\big)

and therefore

\displaystyle\int_{A_{n}}^{\infty}x\big(1-F_{n}(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3)/2}\big).

Hence by
5
,

\displaystyle\int_{|x|>A_{n}}x^{2}f_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n})

(5.7)

and by inserting

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{|Z|>A_{n}\}=2\big(1-\Phi(A_{n})\big)\leq 2\phi(A_{n})=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3)/2}\big)

(5.8)

into
5.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{|x|>A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\log\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n}).

In order to evaluate
D(T_{n})
, we thus only have to examine (with
L(u):=u\log(u)
)

\begin{split}\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\log\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x&=\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}L\Big(\frac{f_{n}(x)}{\phi(x)}\Big)\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}L\big(1+u_{m}(x)+v_{n}(x)\big)\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

(5.9)

where

\displaystyle u_{m}(x)=\frac{\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)}{\phi(x)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{n}(x)=\frac{f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)}{\phi(x)}.

By
Theorem

1.2
,

\begin{split}\big|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)\big|&\leq(1+|x|)^{-(m-1)}R_{n}(x)\\
&\leq(1+|x|)^{-(m-1)}n^{-(m-2)/2}\log(n)r_{n}\end{split}

(5.10)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
n
sufficiently large. Here,
r_{n}=n^{(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-1}\,\sup_{x}|R_{n}(x)|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(1)
.

There exists
C_{m}>0
, such that
y\mapsto(1+y)^{m-1}\phi(y)
is decreasing for
y\geq C_{m}
and thus its inverse is increasing. Additionally, for
|x|\leq C_{m}
, we bound

(1+|x|)^{-(m-1)}\phi(x)^{-1}\leq c(m).

Therefore, we estimate

(1+|x|)^{-(m-1)}\phi(x)^{-1}\leq(1+A_{n})^{-(m-1)}\phi(A_{n})^{-1}

for all
|x|\leq A_{n}
and for
n
sufficiently large. Hence,

\displaystyle|v_{n}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq(1+|x|)^{-(m-1)}\phi(x)^{-1}n^{-(m-2)/2}\log(n)r_{n}

\displaystyle\leq c(m)A_{n}^{-(m-1)}e^{A_{n}^{2}/2}\,n^{-(m-2)/2}\log(n)r_{n}

\displaystyle=c(m)\big((m-2)\log n+(m-3)\log\log n+\rho_{n}\big)^{-(m-1)/2}\big(\log n\big)^{(m-3)/2}

(5.11)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot e^{\rho_{n}/2}\log(n)r_{n}

\displaystyle\leq c(m)e^{\rho_{n}/2}r_{n}.

Next, we choose the sequence
(\rho_{n})
in a way that the last expression goes to 0. Thus,
{|v_{n}(x)|<1/4}
holds for
|x|\leq A_{n}
and
n
sufficiently large.

Recall
\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)=\phi(x)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-3}q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}
from
1.5
. For even
r
,
q_{r}
factorizes as
\phi
multiplied with an even polynomial of degree
2r
. The coefficients of
q_{r}
are functions of the moments
\mu_{3},\dots,\mu_{r+2}
and can thus be bounded by
c(r)
. As above,
q_{r}=0
for uneven
r\in\mathbb{N}
. So

\begin{split}|u_{m}(x)|=\frac{\big|\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)\big|}{\phi(x)}\leq c(m)\big(1+|x|^{2m-6}\big)n^{-1/2}\leq c(m)A_{n}^{2m-6}n^{-1/2},\end{split}

(5.12)

where the last inequality only holds for
|x|\leq A_{n}
. In particular
|u_{m}(x)|<1/4
holds for
|x|\leq A_{n}
and
n
sufficiently large.
Next, by Lemma
A.7
,

L(1+u+v)=L(1+u)+v+\vartheta_{1}uv+\vartheta_{2}v^{2}

for
|u|\leq 1/4,|v|\leq 1/4
and
|\vartheta_{j}|\leq 2
depending on
u
and
v
.
Inserting
u=u_{m}(x)
and
v=v_{n}(x)
, shows how to eliminate
v_{n}(x)
from
5.9
. This produces an error not exceeding

|D_{1}|+2D_{2}+2D_{3},

where

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}\big(f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x,

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}\big|u_{m}(x)\big|\big|f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x

and

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}\frac{\big(f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)\big)^{2}}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x.

For even
r\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
q_{r}
factorizes as
\phi
multiplied with a sum of Hermite polynomials
H_{k}
for
k>0
(see
2.13
,
2.16
and
1.6
). Now

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(x)H_{k}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(x)H_{k}(x)H_{0}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0

for all
k>0
yields

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}q_{r}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=0

(5.13)

for all even
r=1,\dots,m-3
. As
q_{r}=0
for uneven
r\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\ \big(f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x=0.

Thus,

|D_{1}|=\Big|\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\big(f_{n}(x)-\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)\big)\,\mathrm{d}x\Big|\leq\int_{|x|>A_{n}}f_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\big|\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x,

where the first integral is of order
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n})
by
5.7
. Using
5.12
and
5.8
, the second integral is bounded by

\begin{split}&\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\big|\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\quad\leq c(m)\int_{|x|>A_{n}}|x|^{2m-6}n^{-1/2}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathbb{P}\{|Z|>A_{n}\}\\
&\quad=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3)/2}\big).\end{split}

(5.14)

For
m>3
, we have used
5
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{2(2m-6)}
and for
m=3
the order is true because in that case the order of the first term is smaller than the second one.
Therefore,
{|D_{1}|=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n})}
. By
5.12
and
5.10
,

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}A_{n}^{2m-6}n^{-1/2}(1+|x|)^{-(m-1)}n^{-(m-2)/2}\log(n)r_{n}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq c(m)A_{n}^{2m-5}n^{-(m-1)/2}\log(n)r_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(n^{-(m-1)/2}(\log n)^{(2m-3)/2}).

Similarly as in
5
, we obtain for all
|x|\leq A_{n}

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}(1+|x|)^{-2(m-1)}\phi(x)^{-1}n^{-(m-2)}(\log n)^{2}r_{n}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq c(m)A_{n}^{-(2m-3)}\phi(A_{n})^{-1}n^{-(m-2)}(\log n)^{2}r_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle=c(m)\big((m-2)\log n+(m-3)\log\log n+\rho_{n}\big)^{-(2m-3)/2}\big(\log n\big)^{(m-3)/2}\,e^{\rho_{n}/2}

\displaystyle\quad\cdot n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{2}r_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c(m)(\log n)^{-(m-4)/2}n^{-(m-2)/2}r_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n}).

Thus, eliminating
v_{n}(x)
from
5.9
leads to

D(T_{n})=\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}L\big(1+u_{m}(x)\big)\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n}).

For
m=3
,
u_{m}(x)=0
and
5.1
holds. Hence, assume
m\geq 4
from now on. By Taylor expansion around
u=0
,

\displaystyle L(1+u)

\displaystyle=u+\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}u^{k}+\vartheta u^{m-1}

for some
\vartheta\geq 0
, depending on
u
and
m
that can be bounded by
c(m)
for all
|u|\leq 1/4
.
Inserting
u_{m}
yields

\displaystyle\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}L\big(1+u_{m}(x)\big)\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}u_{m}(x)^{k}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+c(m)\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}\big|u_{m}(x)\big|^{m-1}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=:E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}

for
n
large enough. By
5.13
and
5.14
,

\displaystyle|E_{1}|=\bigg|\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|=\bigg|\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\big|\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)\big|\,\mathrm{d}x=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3)/2}\big)

and by
5.12

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big|u_{m}(x)\big|^{m-1}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq c(m)n^{-(m-1)/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\big(1+|x|^{2m-6}\big)^{m-1}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\mathcal{O}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}\big).

By
5.12
and
5
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{2(2m-6)}
, the integral in
E_{2}
can be extended to the whole real line at the expense of an error not exceeding

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\int_{|x|>A_{n}}u_{m}(x)^{k}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq c(m)\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}n^{-k/2}\int_{|x|>A_{n}}\big(1+|x|^{2m-6}\big)^{k}\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-1)/2}(\log n)^{-(m-3)/2}\big).

In summary,

\displaystyle D(T_{n})

\displaystyle=\int_{|x|\leq A_{n}}L\big(1+u_{m}(x)\big)\phi(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\big(\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)\big)^{k}}{\phi(x)^{k-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n}).

Recalling
\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)=\sum_{r=1}^{m-3}q_{r}(x)n^{-r/2}
, we get

\displaystyle\big(\phi^{q}_{m-1,n}(x)-\phi(x)\big)^{k}=\sum_{l=1}^{k(m-3)}n^{-l/2}\sum q_{r_{1}}(x)\dots q_{r_{k}}(x)

where the inner sum runs over all positive integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\leq m-3
with
r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}=l
. So

\displaystyle D(T_{n})=\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\sum_{l=1}^{k(m-3)}n^{-l/2}\sum\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{r_{1}}(x)\dots q_{r_{k}}(x)}{\phi(x)^{k-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n}).

Here, all summands with uneven
l=r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}
vanish as
q_{r}=0
for uneven
r
.
Additionally, all summands with
l>m-2
will be absorbed by the remainder
\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n})
which yields

\begin{split}D(T_{n})&=\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\sum_{l=1,~l\text{ even}}^{m-2}n^{-l/2}\sum\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{r_{1}}(x)\dots q_{r_{k}}(x)}{\phi(x)^{k-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n})\\
&=\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor(m-2)/2\rfloor}n^{-l}\sum\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{r_{1}}(x)\dots q_{r_{k}}(x)}{\phi(x)^{k-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}(\Delta_{m,n})\end{split}

(5.15)

where the inner sum runs over all positive integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\leq m-3
such that
r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}=l
in the first line and
r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}=2l
in the second line. Define

\displaystyle c_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{m-2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\sum\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{r_{1}}(x)\dots q_{r_{k}}(x)}{\phi(x)^{k-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=2}^{2l}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k(k-1)}\sum\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{r_{1}}(x)\dots q_{r_{k}}(x)}{\phi(x)^{k-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Here, the inner sum in the first line runs over all positive integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\leq m-3
such that
r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}=2l
and the the inner sum in the second line runs over all positive integers
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}
such that
r_{1}+\dots+r_{k}=2l
. The fact
2\leq k\leq 2l\leq m-2
implies the second identity.
Note that

c_{1}=\frac{(-1)^{2}}{2}\sum_{r_{1}+r_{2}=2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{r_{1}}(x)q_{r_{2}}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{q_{1}(x)q_{1}(x)}{\phi(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x=0

and thus,
Theorem

5.1
follows from
5.15
.
∎

6
Rate of convergence for Edgeworth expansions in the central limit theorem in total variation distance for self-normalized sums

The main goal of this section is to prove
Theorem

1.5
by examining the total variation distance between
F_{n}
and
\Phi^{Q}_{m,n}
. By
1.8
, this is equivalent to bounding the
L^{1}
norm of the difference of the corresponding densities.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular,
1
is satisfied and
\mathbb{E}|X_{1}|^{2m}<\infty
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 3
. Then there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N
, the statistics
T_{n}
have densities
f_{n}
that satisfy

\|f_{n}-\phi^{q}_{m,n}\|_{L^{1}}=\begin{smallmatrix}\!{\mathcal{O}}\!\end{smallmatrix}\big(n^{-(m-2)/2}\big).

(6.1)

Together with
Proposition

4.2
and
1.8
,
Theorem

6.1
implies
Theorem

1.5
. On our route to proving
Theorem

6.1
, we first show the following proposition. For any
h\in\mathbb{R}
, let
f(\raisebox{-2.15277pt}{\scalebox{2}{$\cdot$}}+h)
denote the function
x\mapsto f(x+h)
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Assume that
X_{1}
is symmetric, the distribution of
X_{1}
is non-singular and
